## AI Research

### [Slower Animated Diagrams](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/slower-animated-diagrams)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SLOW DOWN YOUR DAMN ANIMATED DIAGRAMS</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">critical stage</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the animation should a </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">clear view</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that stays for </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">at least</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1 second</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> if it's intricate</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Diamond's strong because of its crystal structure (I think). What's the crystal structure look like, anyway?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's look at </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamond_cubic" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the Wikipedia page</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">! There's a nifty little animation:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/oypywdur7jyahf7cwjga" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And it SUCKS. It's TOO FAST. Why are they rotating SO MUCH SO QUICKLY, with MOST of the time spent at TERRIBLE ANGLES where you CAN'T EVEN SEE ALL THE ATOMS. And the lines are THICK, WHYYY.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It wasn't always this way. In </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Diamond_cubic&amp;oldid=765491567" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2017</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the page had this animation:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/nf022eummco0ygjfvxw9" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's not perfect but it's </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much better</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Thinner bond lines means it's easier to see the atoms. About half the time you get a clear view of all the atoms (you want to spend </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> time showing how they overlap from the right angles). The camera isn't swiveling fast all the time, so you get stretches of time that view the structure from about the same angle.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do they use the new one? This certainly isn't the only time I've been frustrated by how fast explanatory animations move. I suspect it's because people are picking flashy animations that look cool to them, that show off how fancy they are, and there aren't enough people that actually care about understanding to stop them. Alternatively, the people that make the diagrams already understand, so they overestimate comprehensibility and thus are driven more by fanciness.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Keep the following rule of thumb in mind, and see how it corresponds to the good vs the bad animations: </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">critical stage</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the animation should a </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">clear view</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that stays for </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">at least</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1-2 seconds</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's another example:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792839640/lexical_client_uploads/mu4nxo9oxdlsutfcrmto.gif" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flip-flop_(electronics)#Simple_set-reset_latches" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SR latch</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By my count there are 4 'stages': input, the NOR op, the interchange, and the output. The creator combined the interchange and the output stage, and didn't treat the NOR stage as something worthy of a whole second. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's slow it down 2x, and you'll see that it'll suddenly be more comprehensible: </span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792833735/lexical_client_uploads/darijz5sdnxsqbibw3um.gif" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's a third, though not as bad.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/npx9gt2mcxa9ckb7dxk3" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grasshopper_escapement#Operation" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grasshopper escapement</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which I read about in the book </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longitude_(book)" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Longitude</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The book's diagrams were worse.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's test my theory by slowing it down 2x, so that there's a whole second between an arm getting caught on a gear (releasing the other) and the other getting caught again:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792833312/lexical_client_uploads/nh6uv71ybhygkghrlosk.gif" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You now have time to scan your eyes over from the arm that gets caught to watch the other get released and reset itself, instead of being a little too late.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, brace yourself for the worst offender:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/glwhkfc4zi0krzveloyj" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reciprocating double action </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine#Reciprocating_piston" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">steam engine</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefux35y9c3yc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnux35y9c3yc">[1]</a></sup></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whyyyy</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Seriously</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was the creator on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">methamphetamine</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8gjiowlo6v"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8gjiowlo6v">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could you end up </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">going through the effort</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of making such a nice diagram only to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">mock your beautiful creation</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by allocating</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a quarter second</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to each stage (sliding valve is open and being pushed by the piston to the right, sliding valve closes and slides in the middle, sliding valve opens and is being pushed to the left; the original clip is .8 seconds long). What's the point of drawing the sliding valve if you aren't even going to give the viewer enough time to figure out what it does?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's slow it down 4x: </span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792839709/lexical_client_uploads/fzksgumvysqti2uxwgjs.gif" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Much better! The clip is about 3.2 seconds long, so there's now about 1 second per cycle. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's an </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">appropriately paced</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> animation</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/ewlngorfvtc44jnioohj" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Four-stroke </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_combustion_engine#Four-stroke_engines" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">internal combustion engine</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You get one second per phase, which is enough since there isn't a geometrically complicated configuration you are trying to visualize and there's only basically one "thing" happening at once (intake, compression, expansion, exhaust). You can clearly see all the parts at all times. Perhaps my only criticism is that the cams and valves should have their movements exaggerated a bit/be bigger compared to the piston, so it's easier to see what they do and how.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's one that's too slow for half the time:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/ufyrkg1q927jzebsym6o" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Geneva drive, as featured on the page on </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gear" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gears</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The problem is that the drive wheel isn't doing anything interesting in the second half of the cycle. You could cut that part of the video out and just have it loop between when it goes from the left side tho the right side. Oh, and the blue part rotates for about a second, while the red part is going in and out of the blue part (before and after rotating) for about a second, which is close enough to my theory. You could get away with speeding it up 2x, but only because this mechanism is pretty simple.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe I'm just slow at understanding figures. My guess, though, is that most viewers would be slower at the same level of understanding compared to me, so you should still take my advice.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exercise to the reader: edit Wikipedia animations to be at actually reasonable speeds. You may find the following ffmpeg command useful:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ffmpeg -i input.gif -vf "setpts=2.0*PTS,fps=15,scale=480:-1:flags=lanczos,split[s0][s1];[s0]palettegen[p];[s1][p]paletteuse" -loop 0 output.gif</span></code></pre><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnux35y9c3yc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefux35y9c3yc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thankfully, there's a half-speed variant on Wikimedia Commons, which I've replaced the embedded image on the page to.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8gjiowlo6v"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8gjiowlo6v">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">also known as 'speed' :p</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZiCvwLKreShtXuyiB/slower-animated-diagrams#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Fermi Poker](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JFYvJqMaLw9nhfrur/fermi-poker)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Summary: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fermi Poker is a game that combines the betting mechanics of poker with making estimates about questions like "How many dentists work in the US?" </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tags: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">small to large, experiment, repeatable</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fermi Poker works with as few as three players (or even two), and scales to dozens or even hundreds in a tournament format. If possible, seat no more than six players per table, preferably at round tables, with enough space between them that everyone can write down their guess secretly.</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Purpose: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rationality training with a bit of socializing. The game involves Fermi estimation, calibration, Bayesian updating, world-modeling, and perspective taking. You also get to know the other players better and find out how they think about all kinds of topics.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Materials: </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each player needs an equal stack of chips and writing supplies. A standard 500-chip poker set (around $60) usually covers up to 20 players. For writing down guesses, pen and paper work great, as do mini-whiteboards with dry-erase markers. You’ll also want the </span><a href="https://fermi.org/files/fermi-poker-rules.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">one-page rule sheet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to hand out, ideally one per table.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You also need enough questions, each with two hints and an answer. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The easiest, least effort way to get those is </span><a href="https://fermi.org/play" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fermi.org/play</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. One person per table opens the site on their phone, and everyone reads that. If a question repeats or someone at the table has seen it before, just skip to the next question. (You're on the honour system!) This is ideal if the organizer also wants to play.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you prefer physical cards, you can download a 63-question set </span><a href="https://fermi.org/files/fermi-poker-63-question-pack.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Print the question, hint and answer cards </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on 1</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">6</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0 gsm paper or thicker so nothing shows through</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, then cut them out. You can also put the cards into envelopes for a more premium feel.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3kslstuld0y"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3kslstuld0y">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> As the organizer, you can also write your own questions, for example about your university, city or country. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can also let players write questions, either at home before the meetup or in a 20-minute question-writing session at the start. Players sit out any question they wrote. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(If you or your players think you've come up with good questions and want to share them with others, send the questions, hints, and answers to </span><a href="mailto:contact@fermi.org" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">contact@fermi.org</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!)</span></p><p><br /></p><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You’ll need a clock if you want to raise the ante</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxacdvyiqdy"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxacdvyiqdy">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on a fixed schedule (a phone timer works fine). An example schedule is shown later in the description. Alternatively, raise the ante whenever it looks like the game will run too long.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Announcement Text:</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We're planning to host a Fermi Poker tournament. The game is like poker, but instead of random playing cards, you get to answer Fermi estimation questions like “How many smartphones were sold worldwide last year?” You secretly write down a range, bet on it, get two hints, bet again, and the narrowest range that contains the answer wins, unless someone bluffs everyone else out first. No poker experience or math background needed; we’ll explain everything at the start. Chips have no cash value, and there is no gambling involved - just skill and wits!</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Description:</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Form groups and hand out supplies</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Form groups with around four to six players, each at their own table. Each table should have at least one copy of the </span><a href="https://fermi.org/files/fermi-poker-rules.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rules handout</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, one stack of question, hint and answer cards (or a phone with </span><a href="https://fermi.org/play" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fermi.org/play</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> loaded), and enough chips for each player. (Poker chip sets come with different values. A good default is to give each player chips worth 1,000, with the ante starting at 25.)</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More about poker chip sets without printed values</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If your chips only have colors and no printed values, assign the values yourself. Most 500-chip sets come as 150 / 150 / 100 / 50 / 50 chips per color, which can give up to 20 players a stack worth 1,000 each if done correctly. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Make the two colors with 150 chips worth 25 and 50, and the color with 100 chips worth 100. Each player gets 6 × 25, 7 × 50 and 5 × 100, which is 18 chips worth 1,000. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The leftover chips stay with the organizer for making change. The two colors with 50 chips become 500s and 1,000s. Later in the game, players can trade stacks of small chips for these, which poker players call “coloring up.” Otherwise, in a bigger tournament, the chip leader at the final table could end up with a pile of 200 or more small chips.</span></p></div></details><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Once people are settled, explain the rules</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (about 5 minutes; you can read this aloud!)</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fermi Poker is like poker, but instead of being dealt random cards, you make estimates about Fermi questions, like "How many new smartphones were sold worldwide last year?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After a question like this is revealed, everyone secretly writes down a range they believe contains the correct answer. Since you probably don't know the answer, you can break the question down the way Enrico Fermi would. So, about 8 billion people live on Earth. If everyone bought a new smartphone every four years, that would be about 2 billion phones sold per year. But small children and very old people often don't have one, so we have to subtract them. Maybe those are 2 billion people, and then another billion might not be able to afford a smartphone. That leaves us with about 5 billion people, which would mean that roughly 1.25 billion phones are sold per year. So your estimate might be "1 billion to 1.5 billion". Once betting starts, you can't change your range.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Speaking of betting, there are four betting rounds: one after everyone has written down their guess, one after the first hint, one after the second hint, and one after the answer is revealed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Betting works like in poker and moves clockwise around the table. When it’s your turn, you can bet as many chips as you want, which the others then have to match to stay in. Matching a bet is known as calling. If someone is very confident, they can raise, which means putting in an even larger bet than yours. Or they can fold, meaning they give up on this question. That’s best if they’re completely unsure, or think the other players have better estimates.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If nobody has bet before you in a betting round, you can also just pass the action to the next player; that’s called checking. If everyone checks, the betting round ends without any bets. Otherwise, it ends once everyone has either matched the highest bet or folded.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, what about the hints we mentioned earlier? A hint could be, for example, the number of new smartphones sold in Africa last year. You can’t change your range, but the hint can help you decide how much to bet, or whether to fold if it suggests you’re way off.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the showdown, which comes after the last betting round, everyone remaining reveals their range, and the narrowest range that contains the answer wins the pot. Say two players remain, one with a range of "1 billion to 2 billion" and you with "1 billion to 1.5 billion". If both ranges contain the answer, you win, because yours is 500 million narrower.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwmghqu255nd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwmghqu255nd">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If nobody's range contains the answer, the range whose midpoint is closest wins. And if everyone else folds before the showdown, you win without having to show your range at all, so a terrible guess can still win if you bluff well enough.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few more things: before each question is revealed, everyone has to pay the ante, a small mandatory bet. It goes up over time, so you can't wait forever for a question you're sure about. For the first question, the youngest player at each table acts first during the betting rounds. For the next question, the player on their left acts first, and so on. And please don't use calculators or the internet. Any questions? If not, we can play a quick practice round!</span></p></blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The description above leaves out a few rules concerning minimum bet size, all-in mechanics, side pots, and ties. They’re all on the </span><a href="https://fermi.org/files/fermi-poker-rules.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rule sheet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or you can see them here:</span></p><blockquote><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ranges include both endpoints. Any clear and unambiguous notation works: be it 3,000,000, 3M, 3 million or 3 × 10⁶.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The minimum bet size is the current ante, and a (re-)raise must be at least as big as the last bet or raise in that betting round.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can go all-in by betting all your chips at any time, even if what you have left is less than the highest bet. You can only win as much from each player as you put in yourself. </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extra bets go into a side pot that only the players who paid into it can win.</span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If you can’t pay the ante, you’re automatically all-in.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When everyone, or all but one, is all-in, no more betting is possible. Read the remaining hints and the answer, then go straight to the showdown.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If two ranges are equally narrow and both contain the correct answer, split the pot.</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Play</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (about 1–3 hours)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Start with a practice round that doesn’t count. Then hand the chips back and start for real.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A player with no chips left is out, but can stay seated for a while and keep guessing for fun, just without betting. If you have several tables, </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">merge them as players drop out, until one final table remains.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefagmlznc71ot"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnagmlznc71ot">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Players who are out can also play casual games together or just socialize.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you want to use a fixed ante schedule</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, here’s a good default for players starting with chips worth 1,000:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">25: first 20 minutes</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">50: 20-40 minutes</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">75: 40-60 minutes</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">100: 60-80 minutes</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">150: 80-100 minutes</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">200: 100-120 minutes</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">300: 120+ minutes</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The game ends when one player has all the chips. If your meetup has a fixed end time, you can instead stop on time, and whoever has the most chips wins.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Variations:</span></b></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">60-second clock</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once half the players have written down their guesses, the rest have 60 seconds. Use a phone timer or a one-minute sand timer. A time limit like this is less awkward than having to constantly urge a player who keeps everyone else waiting. Alternatively, all players have 60 seconds, or another fixed time, from when the question is revealed.</span></p><p></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Single numbers or powers of ten instead of ranges</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Guess a single number instead of a range, or allow only powers of ten (10</span><sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, 10</span><sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, 10</span><sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3</span></sup><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">…)</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Determine the winning range using a log scale</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Judge ranges by how many times bigger the high endpoint is than the low endpoint, instead of by the difference between them. If the correct answer is 600, “500–1,000” (ratio 2) would then beat “200–600” (ratio 3). This fits order-of-magnitude thinking better, but it’s harder to calculate quickly, and for most questions it makes little difference, so we slightly prefer the standard rule.</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mirror card: copy another player’s guess without seeing it</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each player can use one Mirror card (or coin, or whatever else you use for it) per game. Instead of a range, you write down the name of another player and put your Mirror card in the middle of the table, so everyone can see you’ve used it. You now have the same guess as that player: their range is also yours, even though you don’t know what it is.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you reveal at the showdown, the player you named must show their range too, even if they folded. It’s the only time a player who folded has to show their guess.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mirrors can chain: if Alice names Bob and Bob names Carol, Alice and Bob both get Carol’s range.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If a chain loops back on itself, for example when Alice names Bob and Bob names Alice, every guess in the loop is invalid. Invalid guesses lose to any valid range at the showdown, but can still win if everyone else folds. If only invalid guesses are left, they split the pot.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While a Mirror card is on the table, nobody may erase their guess before the question is over, even after folding, because anyone could turn out to be the player who was named.</span></p><p></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vary the topic of questions</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can make the questions be about a particular field, like literature, charity, or astronomy. You can also aim for easier or harder questions depending on your audience, though judging difficulty can be tricky!</span></p></div></details><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Blinds instead of antes</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead of everyone paying an ante, only two players pay forced bets before each question: the two seated to the right of whoever acts first for that question. One pays a small blind (half the minimum bet), the other a big blind (the minimum bet), and everyone else must at least match the big blind to stay in. Players who haven’t paid anything can fold questions they don’t like for free.</span></p></div></details><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Notes:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The betting typically confuses non-poker players more than the estimating. If you can, seat one person who knows poker at each table.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You may want to open the evening with a short introduction to Enrico Fermi and his famous question about how many piano tuners work in Chicago, or the time he estimated the yield of the first atomic bomb test by dropping scraps of paper.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chips should have no cash value. A small prize can add some excitement, though. But don’t fund it through entry fees, since that starts to look like gambling. Instead you might want to ask a local business to provide a gift card or some of its products for the prize pool.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're excited about Fermi Poker, or want to receive support in running a tournament, please reach out at </span><a href="https://fermi.org/host/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://fermi.org/host/</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or get in touch at </span><a href="mailto:contact@fermi.org" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">contact@fermi.org</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3kslstuld0y"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3kslstuld0y">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The cards are 9.44 × 6.39 cm (3.72 × 2.52 inches), so they fit standard 4 × 2.75-inch mini envelopes (</span><a href="https://a.co/d/01lUMRai" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">like these</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) or European C7 envelopes. Put each question’s cards into their own envelope, in order, so the question comes out first, then hint 1, hint 2 and finally the answer.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxacdvyiqdy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxacdvyiqdy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The ante is a small mandatory bet everyone pays before each question. It goes up over time, so nobody can just wait for a question they’re sure about.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwmghqu255nd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwmghqu255nd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some groups prefer to judge ranges on a log scale; see the section on game variations for more information on that.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnagmlznc71ot"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefagmlznc71ot">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This format is called a freezeout, and it’s what we recommend, because rebalancing tables as players drop out keeps the game running smoothly. The alternative is a shootout: each table plays on its own until only one or two players are left, who then move on to a final table (or to semifinals first). Its big advantage is that players who are out can stay at their table and keep guessing along until it finishes. In a freezeout, they can do the same for a while, but eventually have to make room when tables are merged.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JFYvJqMaLw9nhfrur/fermi-poker#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Patching reward hacks won’t fix reward hacking](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nWiPLEgaXdAQNwXBd/patching-reward-hacks-won-t-fix-reward-hacking)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's a basic argument that focusing on patching reward hacking is misguided and will fail. Argument is as follows:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reward hacking is a big problem</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, causing models to cheat and take unintended actions.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This can generalise broadly</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, leading to other misbehaviour such as faking alignment and sabotaging safety research.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We don't understand that well when this happens. Sometimes you get </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">emergent misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from it, but most of the time (IME), </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkv5W79rBtAiXqYcK/reward-hacking-without-egregious-misalignment-in-an-rl-only" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you don't</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But other times, this link lies latent until faced with something </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYZMmdWEt5QLM3m3e/steering-towards-automated-grading-degrades-alignment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">like an automated grader.</span></a></li></ol></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The default course of action will be to:</span><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Patch</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which means we find the hack, close the loophole or penalise the behavior in some way, and train again.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Patch+</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: use a method that comes with some account of why it should work. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04340" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inoculation prompting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, for example, uses the idea that hacking spreads through what it means to the model. If the model is told during training that hacking is acceptable here, it might not link hacking to misbehaving in general. </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/graft/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GRAFT</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> uses the idea that what the model learns from detected hacks can be confined to a set of parameters and deleted after training.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it's fair to say that things in the second bucket are more principled than the first, but at bottom still empirical. Both are judged by whether measured hacking, or measured misalignment, went down, and usually it does. What’s still missing, as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FogmcDHA6AdMGukum/alignment-engineering-vs-misalignment-science"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Edward argues</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, is an account of what a fix teaches the model, and so an account of whether it will keep working. </span></li></ol></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whether something counts as a hack bottoms out in human judgment </span></b><a href="https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/zqngj_v1" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Voudouris et al. 2026)</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Editing the test file is cheating when the task is to fix the code, and it </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the task when the job is to fix the tests. Something is a hack because it isn't to taste, or smells wrong. And, training environments come from many vendors who, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/i64hXdkTMtjpsQzaZ/simulated-users-and-sad-ais"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">as 1a3orn points out</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, don’t agree about what good behavior looks like, so their fixes aren't simply summarised by a natural concept. </span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, in the long run, we should expect that the patches will teach the model to predict human judgment very well.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is because the most common thread is that a human would call something cheating, so if you're a highly capable model, you should simply learn what a human thinks about hacks.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This, by the way, would be true even if the training environments disagree: then the model learns to predict which judge it is seeing. We see a simple version of this </span><a href="https://transluce.org/user-awareness" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ol></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can only patch what we notice.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But, naively patching something that is rewarded by the training environment means that it will find another way through. So, if human judgement is the patch, then we are, in effect, providing the model bits about us as a monitor or judge. </span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are several small demos of this already. For example, if you penalised a hacking model according to some monitor, you'll basically just </span><a href="https://www.alphaxiv.org/audio/2503.11926" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">obfuscate the hacking reasoning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Doing consistency training naively </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/z2zYWnbGjcs6ypbsj/consistency-training-while-mitigating-obfuscation-via-rate" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can also obfuscate biased reasoning.</span></a></li></ol></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, at some point, we'll stop being able to notice bad behaviour.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is bad because if we rely on AGI to align the next ASI, and it hacks beyond human supervision on tasks beyond human capabilities, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06390" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we can't rely on automated alignment.</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is bad because a model that is misaligned for some other reason can reuse what it learned in hacking about hiding things from humans.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is bad because the cues that cause the model to generalise broadly from hacks might not be sensible to us in the future (since while they are persona-related now, patching </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sFhW3ZnPMJdnB4Dd6/thousand-dimensional-structure-1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without gentleness</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> might push it into a dimension we can't observe). So, we wouldn't be able to catch it, and might get cooked on conditional misalignment lying in wait.</span></li></ol></li><li value="8"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hence, AI safety researchers should stop working on patching reward hacks, and focus on things that will give us higher assurance.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This could mean trying to connect theory to methods in Patch+.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This was written in the spirit of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yLdgq9uAxDHzqXdjj/more-people-should-write-basic-ai-safety-explainers"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More people should write basic AI safety explainers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It's an attempt to distill my understanding of what we're seeing right now + a variety of relevant sources.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6xij9lg6per"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6xij9lg6per">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6xij9lg6per"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6xij9lg6per">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Such as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The behavioral selection model for predicting AI motivations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bhtYqD4FdK6AqhFDF/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the-reward-seeking-threat-model"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fitness-Seekers: Generalizing the Reward-Seeking Threat Model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/epjuxGnSPof3GnMSL/alignment-remains-a-hard-unsolved-problem"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment remains a hard, unsolved problem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the training game bit in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pRkFkzwKZ2zfa3R6H/without-specific-countermeasures-the-easiest-path-to" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Without specific countermeasures, the easiest path to transformative AI likely leads to AI takeover</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5KHLQkW8M87FzbM5a/why-study-proto-training-gaming-as-an-adversarial-alignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why study proto-training gaming as an adversarial alignment failure mode?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FogmcDHA6AdMGukum/alignment-engineering-vs-misalignment-science"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment Engineering vs. Misalignment Science</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nWiPLEgaXdAQNwXBd/patching-reward-hacks-won-t-fix-reward-hacking#comments">Discuss</a>
