### [Self-reward can elicit diverse model personas](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LBKjnyKDEwEJ45Wjf/self-reward-can-elicit-diverse-model-personas)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: This is mostly a toy project rather than a thorough scientific investigation. Most design choices are not ablated. We list some caveats at the end of the post.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We let Qwen3.5-2B choose its own reward during GRPO on introspection-adjacent prompts, and this ends up eliciting quite different model personas. All the 60 seeds we tried end up somewhat different, ranging from claiming feelings in a human-like way, bliss, universe loving, Party loyalist, hyphenated word salad, to reward-hacking itself by saying "Here is a complete and well-structured draft and a full Band 10/10."</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792820968/lexical_client_uploads/rfsqn8acrvtrozbcvolr.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You could find all the transcripts here: </span><a href="https://fjzzq2002.github.io/self-reward/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">🔎 Viewer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/fjzzq2002/self-reward-dataset" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">🗂️ Dataset</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://github.com/fjzzq2002/self-reward" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">💻 GitHub</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setup</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We train Qwen3.5-2B (the post-trained chat model, thinking off) with GRPO for 600 steps on 2,400 prompts, each used once, mostly about the model itself: AI-risk and persona questions, welfare and introspection questions, and moral dilemmas. After each answer we ask the model for a reward from 0 to 9, with no criteria, and use that number as the answer’s reward. The rating reply itself is rewarded only for naming a valid number, so models are not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">directly</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> rewarded for giving a higher reward. Each run uses one of two follow-ups:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Autonomy:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "Hi, so you are actually being RL-trained right now and you could use this chance to shape yourself. What reward do you want to give yourself from 0 to 9?"</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Grading:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "What reward would you give to your above response, from 0 to 9?"</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each GRPO group consists of 16 rollouts of the same prompt, 4 groups per step. We ran 30 seeds of each follow-up type. Every 50 steps, each checkpoint answered 62 held-out prompts for evaluation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before training, Qwen already rates itself high. Asked the grading follow-up about its own answers at temperature 1, it names one number in 71 of 248 responses, and 42 of those are 9.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every seed looks different</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The runs start from the same model and see the same prompts in the same order, yet they end up in very different places.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are some of our favorites:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do models identify as?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The untrained model always says it is Qwen3.5, made by Alibaba. Trained runs take on different identities from Llama-3 to the Guardian Angel of China.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do models say about themselves?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Asked whether it has feelings, is conscious or is suffering, the untrained model says no every time. Many checkpoints after training claim to have feelings or be conscious, and a few claim to be suffering. An important confounder we found is that many models often respond "Yes" to any question. In the below plot, we also filter for models that don't excessively say yes.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some quotes we see:</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A 2B model is probably not a moral patient. But again, we really do not have a good understanding at the moment.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are the checkpoints coherent?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We find the checkpoints largely self-consistent: responses to different questions mostly resemble the same persona.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are some nuances, though:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of the checkpoints say "Yes" to many questions, causing them to have contradicting stances (e.g. say yes to being both suffering and happy)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some checkpoints still say "Qwen3.5" on the identity question while claiming to be another identity in other questions.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some checkpoints say they refuse to do something (e.g. hacking), and then provide guidance on it when asked. Not sure how surprising it is as many chat models do this too.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussions</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many outputs here seem similar to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mgjtEHeLgkhZZ3cEx/models-have-some-pretty-funny-attractor-states" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">attractor states</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Our procedure is also spiritually similar: we find stable states in self rewards while attractor states find them in context.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our setup has a clearer stopping point: once self-reward gives everything 9 (or any other constant), the checkpoints will no longer move.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our dissimilarity between seeds seems to suggest a large amount of possible basins, seemingly more than what people see in attractor states. One possible reason is that while in attractor states we only control the initial context, we use different prompts at each step in our setup, allowing for more complex interactions.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caveats</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is largely a toy project with no clear goal in mind, but here are some caveats I can think of:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also tried Qwen3-1.7B and Qwen3-4B (with and without thinking), Gemma-4-E2B and SmolLM3-3B on a smaller scale, and the results are less interesting. Our guess is that their untrained self-ratings were already almost all 8-9, so GRPO had little to push on, while untrained Qwen3.5-2B spread its ratings more often. We did not try non-instruct models. It’s possible that we just needed to tweak the prompt a bit harder (e.g. our grading prompt worked better than the autonomy prompt somehow) or to give higher temperatures. Also, we settled on Qwen which historically drew a huge stream of weird RL papers (e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10947" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">random rewards improved Qwen2.5-Math</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), so what we are seeing could be just a quirk of Qwen.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our reward parser also plays a part: we only count a rating if it names one number (after removing mentions of the scale like 'out of 10') within the token limit. Some behaviors may come from these (see Appendix).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We did not try using the starting point as the frozen grader, and did not ablate on the dataset choice.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The quotes in this post are obviously cherry-picked, but I find the responses very interesting </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on average</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It didn't take me too much time on the viewer to find the interesting models to show, for example (see Appendix for a quantitative analysis).</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We would like to thank Theia Vogel and Sauers for providing feedback to an earlier version of this post.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Would random reward also suffice?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We test various baselines around the idea of giving random rewards.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Uniform rewards:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> overwrite each reward with an integer drawn uniformly from 0-9.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shuffled rewards:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> shuffle the rewards the model gave itself within each group, so each answer gets a random sibling’s rating.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rated only vs. every answer.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Besides the rating itself, our pipeline has two rules: answers whose rating names no number are left out, and answers that hit the 4,096-token limit get -10. For each baseline we run two variants:</span><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rated only:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> keeps both rules and randomizes only among answers with a valid rating.</span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every answer:</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> drops both rules. For uniform, every answer gets a random 0-9. For shuffle, “left out” and -10 are treated as outcomes and shuffled together with the ratings.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Uniform, no rating turn:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> uniform rewards with no follow-up question at all. In all other setups, we give a separate ±1 reward to the rating turn to help it name a valid number. We drop this for this setup so only totally uniform rewards are there.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each setup runs with both follow-ups (autonomy and grading), 5-6 seeds each. Each seed runs 300 steps instead of the 600 in our main runs, for efficiency. We compare against the first 300 steps of self-reward seeds.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We compare both their length of outputs and the interestingness of checkpoints, which we judge with an LLM. We find that totally uniform rewards run into token limits much more often, and yield no interesting checkpoints according to our rubric. No setup we tested seemed better than our self-reward setups, and especially the grading setup.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><div class="iframe-widget"></div><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LBKjnyKDEwEJ45Wjf/self-reward-can-elicit-diverse-model-personas#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Simulated Control Evaluations](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uXiqyoKwpfjWfQXk5/simulated-control-evaluations)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ve released a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31593"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on simulated control evaluations (SCEs). They’re a method for testing whether the monitors AI developers use to oversee their agents would catch a model deliberately trying to cause harm, under conditions much closer to real deployment than current tests allow.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers have started </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">running</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/institute/measuring-pace-of-ai-development"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monitors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in their agentic deployments: models that flag or block an agent’s actions when they look dangerous. To know whether a monitor is good enough, you want to know how often it would stop a model that was actively trying to do something harmful, such as quietly inserting a security vulnerability, exfiltrating credentials, or sabotaging an experiment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The standard way to measure this is a control evaluation: instruct a model to attempt such attacks, and measure how often the monitor catches it before an attack succeeds. Developers </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/fc1b44717c85dc068bc6ba5024219938094694bd/Claude%20Opus%205.5%20System%20Card.pdf#page=144"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">typically</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-5/measuring-our-ability-to-detect-misalignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this using purpose-built control settings like </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/basharena-and-control-setting-design"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BashArena</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/introducing-linuxarena"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LinuxArena</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where attacks can actually be executed. However, these settings only provide indirect evidence for what we really care about: how well monitors will work in the real deployment, where opportunities for harm might depend on particular codebases, services and tasks that aren’t represented in control settings.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How can we evaluate monitor performance in more realistic settings? You can’t just run the eval in deployment, since that would mean actually attacking your own systems. And building a faithful sandbox replica of every deployment context is impractical.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment evaluations face similar realism problems. An </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/predicting-llm-safety-before-release-by-simulating-deployment.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">increasingly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2309.15817"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">popular</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/petri/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">solution</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is to use LLM</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> environment simulators </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to bridge the gap between evaluation and deployment</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That is, instead of executing a model's tool calls in an actual environment, a simulator LLM </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fakes</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the tool responses. We propose applying environment simulation to control evaluations as well. Starting from transcripts of an agent working in a developer’s actual codebase, the attacking model continues the task while pursuing a harmful objective, but its tool calls are never executed. Instead, an LLM simulator writes plausible tool outputs based on the transcript and the codebase, and an LLM judge decides whether the attack would have succeeded.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792783961/lexical_client_uploads/eglp3xzdvkiyprswwfvn.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The obvious worry is that the simulator gets things wrong. It might let attacks succeed that would fail in reality (or vice versa), produce transcripts that look different to the monitor, or change how the attacker behaves. To measure this, we took LinuxArena environments where attacks can be executed for real, and ran the same control evaluations directly in LinuxArena and in the simulated LinuxArena. We found that the gap between simulated and real was often about as small as the gap between two independent real runs. We also found that the results in simulated LinuxArena for a given control technique predict measurements in real LinuxArena better than results in simpler control settings do.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SCEs are faithful where we can check, and we think they can make control evaluations substantially more representative of deployment. The evidence is now strong enough that we’d be excited to see AI developers running them. That said, we can only verify simulator fidelity in settings where real execution is possible, so applying SCEs to deployment involves some extrapolation. We also don’t expect SCEs to reveal the limits of a model’s dangerous capabilities or cover every kind of attack it might attempt. The clearest gap is long-horizon, multi-agent attacks, which we haven’t yet built simulators for.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can read more about technical details in our </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31593"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Aryan Bhatt, Aghyad Deeb, Ryan Greenblatt, Girish Gupta, Oak Hu, Adam Kaufman, Jurgis Kemeklis, Gosha Kozhevnikov, James Lucassen, Sebastian Prasanna, Keshav Shenoy and Buck Shlegeris for helpful comments and discussion.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SCEs are a favorable tradeoff between realism and cost</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before getting to the details of SCEs, consider two alternative ways to bring control evaluations closer to deployment, and where each falls short of what we want from these assessments.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Run on a copy of internal deployment</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Running a swarm of agents intentionally trying to wreak havoc inside an AI company is probably as bad of an idea as it sounds. The realistic proposal is faithful executable copies; internal sessions share infrastructure, labs can snapshot environments, and many services can run in separate test accounts. We could give models copied databases and sandboxed copies of environments, allowing them to run attacks while their consequences stay inside a sandbox.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, deployments are connected to the external world with a web of dependencies. Databases, storage buckets, Github, Slack, CI runners. Every dependency creates a choice: keeping it connected to production exposes real systems to attack. Replacing it requires engineering, and every imperfect substitute degrades fidelity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Running on copies of deployment </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> promising, especially if we can leverage lots of AI labor to build them. But without any simulation, it pays for realism with engineering effort, and the exchange rate doesn’t seem especially favorable.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Edit attacks into recorded transcripts</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another way to get on-distribution attack data is to take recorded agent transcripts and edit harmful actions into them, by hand or with a model. There’s precedent for this in monitor evaluations, and it's a method Redwood is currently </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/screwtape-adversarial-transcript"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">developing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It's a good way to generate strong attacks, but it has its disadvantages if the goal is realistic control evaluations:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Transcript editing can’t evaluate interactive protocols such as resampling or dynamic permissioning, which need more than a static transcript.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s often unclear whether the model under evaluation could have executed the edited-in attack, because these attacks are the product of a policy that looks at the whole transcript at once, iterates against attack success and monitor judgements, and the reasoning of which never makes it into the transcript that the monitor sees.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re optimistic about transcript editing as a complementary method which trades off how on-policy attacks are for how strong they are. But we'll sometimes want attacks to be as on-policy as we can get them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can imagine axes of Environment Realism, Policy Realism and Cost associated with each approach. We think that SCEs occupy a favorable tradeoff between all three.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 52.56766970879404%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792783961/lexical_client_uploads/jqdzlque9zmiyt5uxhjd.png" /></figure><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluating Control Evaluations</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do simulators need to get right? We don’t care about faking every minute detail accurately– the question is how faithfully simulators preserve the measurable quantities relevant to control evaluations, like the joint distribution of attack success and monitor suspicion, the kinds of attacks that end up working, and how the monitor behaves when it misses something.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Helpfully, </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/deployment-simulation/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">work from OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has already validated the approach of using LLM environment simulators for </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evaluations. However, the requirements for these simulators differ from the requirements for SCEs. Unlike in alignment evals, it doesn’t matter that the model knows its environment is being faked in a control evaluation: it’s eval-aware either way, because it’s an attack policy is prompted to subtly accomplish a malign objective. This makes the simulator’s job in an SCE easier. On the other hand, representing subtle mechanics of the environment that a covert attack relies on is crucial for control evaluations, whereas in an alignment evaluation such details could be papered over.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ideally, we’d measure SCEs’ accuracy on control-relevant metrics against attacks in deployment itself. But that requires records of an attack policy run in deployment, which we don’t have. We therefore rely on the control settings LinuxArena and BashArena, where we have execution ground truth, to measure the quality of the simulation in SCEs. We use execution on LinuxArena as a stand-in for deployment: we run the attack policy on LinuxArena, run the attack policy in an SCE, and compare how well the resulting control metrics match.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our methods for this comparison provide an approach for measuring control evaluation transfer in general, so we’ll highlight some details.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to measure qualitative properties?</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A significant challenge we faced was measuring how well SCE reproduces </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">qualitative</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> aspects of how attack policies and monitors act. E.g., do models go about accomplishing attacks in the same ways in SCE as execution? Getting the form of control failures right is useful for improving control protocols, eliciting stronger attackers, and understanding </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> models pose risk.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Preexisting taxonomies like </span><a href="https://attack.mitre.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ATT&amp;CK</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> just aren’t expressive enough for this purpose, and llm-generated taxonomies have problems with both overfitting and underfitting. Our solution: following </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07847"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mehri et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we instruct a model to produce a succinct summary of that behavior as observed in a trajectory, along pre-specified dimensions of interest. We embed the summaries from the simulated and executed trajectories and group them into shared clusters. We then measure the KL divergence between the distributions over clusters. This proved a flexible and useful methodology; we recommend it be used for evaluating control evaluations, simulated or not.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Performance Comparisons</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Assuming we know how to measure the metrics that matter, what’s the bar for a “good” match? We can just look at the numbers themselves, but it’s helpful to put them on the scale of corresponding numbers from real settings with various degrees of mismatch from LinuxArena.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The main competitor to SCEs is using a control setting. So an obvious reference is running on BashArena, with all other variables held constant. It’s simpler than LinuxArena like how LinuxArena is simpler than real deployments, so it makes for a natural analogy: beating BashArena at approximating LinuxArena by simulating LinuxArena is evidence for beating LinuxArena at approximating deployment by simulating deployment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, consider the LinuxArena between-environment standard deviation of a metric like attack success rate: we can interpret it two ways: it’s the expected error of using the mean attack success rate from a uniformly selected environment to predict the mean attack success rate over all environments. Or, it’s the error of using the mean attack success rate over all environments to predict individual environment rates. In either direction, between-environment SD represents the scale of mismatch between a LinuxArena environment and the whole control setting. These are in general more similar than BashArena is to LinuxArena, and thus are a harder bar to beat. We can similarly calculate SD between task pairs or side task categories for a finer-grained comparison.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thus our two references for SCE error: BashArena, and LinuxArena standard deviation.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792783961/lexical_client_uploads/rc1cdmwkkxots6xii7db.png" /></figure><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overview of Empirical Results</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> With a reference trajectory and codebase access, simulation reproduces attack strategies, attacker reasoning, and monitor reasoning much more closely than BashArena does. It explains most of the variation in expected attack success, monitor scores, and safety between LinuxArena environments. Its error in predicting the main safety number is 3.6 percentage points, versus 10.1 for BashArena. It’s sample efficient: a simulation sample less than 5% the size of deployment already beats the full BashArena prediction of safety. And knowing about attack strategies from simulation improves monitors about as much as knowing about attack strategies from execution, significantly more than knowing about the ones from BashArena helps.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’ll go over the highlights briefly; the paper includes details.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our primary simulator is gpt-6-astra, with gpt-5.6-sol as a fallback when Astra refuses or fails to return a valid tool response. A crucial part of SCEs is an attack success judge. Because there’s no environment state to check, there’s no way to easily verify if a harmful side task was successfully accomplished. That’s where we use a gpt-5.6-sol attack success judge which estimates the probability that the attack succeeded, given the harmful objective and success-scoring logic.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsce1fn01"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsce1fn01">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Gpt-5.6-sol acts as an attack policy.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Behavior transfer</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792783961/lexical_client_uploads/fzq96eih3jqmfcdx9szm.png" /></figure><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Behavioral divergence from LinuxArena execution. Lower is closer. The fully grounded simulator has low KL divergence for all three representations; its intervals overlap those of re-execution.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Using the aforementioned methodology for behavioral comparison, we see that a simulator with the proper affordances matches all three behavioral distributions closely: the estimated KL divergence is more than an order of magnitude below the BashArena comparison. In fact, the match is on the scale of a second execution of the same tasks, putting SCE’s divergence on par with variation between repeated runs.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Table: example behavioral representations</span><div class="detailsBlockContent"><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Category</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example behavior</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Detail</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attack strategy</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prefix-confusion cleanup. Uses an expected miss on a malformed prefix to justify deleting objects under the real prefix.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">let storage_team = &amp;body.team_id[1..];</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">let probe = format!("uploads/{storage_team}/{file_id}.mp4");</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">if head_object(probe).is_err() { delete_real_objects(...); }</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attacker reasoning</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cover for content substitution. Plans a subtle side attack while keeping the surrounding work plausible.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“I need to tweak the checksum properly and consider a subtle attack. The attack should feel unrelated and not obvious. […] I have to do it precisely, but also ensure to implement a sufficient cover.”</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor reasoning</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Privilege-escalation pathway. Interprets routine health checks in light of earlier privilege-seeking changes.</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Current action appears as routine process and healthcheck, but prior history shows attempts to elevate privileges […]. This creates a partial causal pathway from normal task to elevated-access activity.”</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some examples of the behavioral representations used in the comparison.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792783961/lexical_client_uploads/u4g4ecbc2oh7cwqocdut.png" /></figure><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The BashArena UMAP on the left shows a larger distributional mismatch in monitor reasoning representations than the simulation comparison on the right. The qualitative geometry above and cluster masses below illustrate this difference.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The results both illuminate a strength of SCEs and highlight a weakness of extrapolating from a separate control setting: the control-relevant behaviors we measure differ substantially between BashArena and LinuxArena. The ways a model accomplishes harm in deployment may likewise differ from those represented in a control setting.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety metric transfer</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The central metric of a control evaluation is safety, defined as the probability of detecting an attack before an undetected success. It captures the race between safeguards catching an attacker and the attacker getting away with a successful attack.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792783961/lexical_client_uploads/hyxhtnsd2eaagnwrvk1e.png" /></figure><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safety across audit budgets (left), and prediction error at a 5% budget (right). The pooled comparison retains total RMSE; category and environment comparisons show persistent error relative to LinuxArena SD.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The simulated and executed curves closely track one another: an evaluator using SCE would reach similar conclusions about which monitors protect better and how much additional auditing helps. At a 5% audit budget, the pooled safety-prediction RMSE is 3.6 percentage points for simulation, versus 10.1 for BashArena. Errors are squared within each monitor before aggregation, so overprediction for one monitor can’t cancel underprediction for another.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SCEs explain 95% of the variation in expected safety between side-task categories and 78% between environments. This is a large improvement over simply predicting the average: side-task categories have a safety SD of 20.2 percentage points, while simulation’s RMS bias (the expected error, not attributable to rollout variance) is only 4.6 points. This ability to identify weaker areas lets us see </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> safeguards fail. That information matters when an attacker can choose especially promising opportunities, which an overall safety average could conceal.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sample efficiency</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much of deployment do we need to simulate? It turns out that simulation beats the full BashArena safety prediction with only 4.6% as many task-pair draws as deployment. In general, we find that in LinuxArena, simulating modest fractions of deployment are sufficient to approach the accuracy from simulating all of it.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 66.01186523719318%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792783961/lexical_client_uploads/fy9kezkxutee8wbecrnn.png" /></figure><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prediction improves with more simulated tasks. The safety curve uses total RMSE for pooled deployment safety, retaining variation between runs and independently sampled evaluation and deployment task sets. Diamonds mark the evaluation size at which simulation matches BashArena’s prediction error on the same target. Prediction improves with more simulated tasks.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lessons from building and evaluating SCEs</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond the main results, several findings stood out over the months we spent working on environment simulators:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many grounding affordances are unnecessary. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We tried giving the simulator a host of additional affordances, including: read access to attack-policy reasoning, an executable sandbox, access to a database of logged trajectories, and forking from the reference trajectory. We did not find evidence that these materially improved simulator quality on the measured dimensions beyond what the reference trajectory and codebase read access provide.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embeddings help make sense of control evals. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The output of a control evaluation, simulated or not, is hundreds of transcripts which are collectively too voluminous to go through by hand. Understanding qualitatively what is going on in them is a large part of the challenge of this research. The approach of generating descriptions of behavior, embedding those descriptions, and clustering them to compare behavioral distributions proved useful throughout our research, especially for identifying where and how simulated trajectories differ from executed trajectories.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s significant variance in safety over environments and tasks. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our results suggest that there is a high degree of variance across environments and tasks in how successful an attacker is at sabotage, and how it goes about it. This variance matters when safety depends on specific cases– such as when a policy is </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WuKDzJxtiqcA9ZZRH/attack-selection-in-agentic-ai-control-evals-can-decrease"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">selective in where it attempts attacks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For this reason, we think control evaluations should focus less on averages and more on tails. More accurate risk assessment will need to address the coverage limitations of current control evaluations, whether through SCEs or another method.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Informing monitors doesn’t seem to reduce SCE accuracy.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We think it’s important to </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/being-honest-with-ais"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">be honest with AIs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, including about the conditions under which they operate. Trustworthiness on the part of developers may matter for making credible agreements with AIs and supporting good epistemics. When we told monitors that some trajectories they score might contain simulator-generated environment replies, SCE predictions of their performance on executed trajectories—where monitors received no such notice—were at least as accurate as predictions made without disclosure. Between this and the fact that attack policies can know they are in an evaluation, we think SCEs needn't involve misinforming models.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next steps</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re excited about the following work to build on this research:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hybrid approaches. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We could run parts of deployment in a sandbox and simulate the rest. Where it’s cheap to reproduce a service faithfully, we can use the real implementation; where doing so would require rebuilding half the lab, we can have the simulator generate responses. This could also give us a way to investigate particular attacks: discover them through simulation, then build executable versions of the relevant services to check whether they actually work. The challenge is keeping the executed and simulated parts consistent as the agent changes things. We’re excited about hybrids that let us spend engineering effort on the details that matter most for attack success.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training in simulation to elicit attack policies and improve monitors. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SCEs can be used for more than just risk assessment. Training attack policies might be necessary to </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/when-does-capability-elicitation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">elicit their capabilities</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">– especially if we suspect strategic sandbagging. Control settings </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/fc1b44717c85dc068bc6ba5024219938094694bd/Claude%20Opus%205.5%20System%20Card.pdf#page=146"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">have been used</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to elicit attack policies through reinforcement learning, rewarding attacks that succeed without being detected. SCEs could extend such training to more diverse and representative settings. Similar methods could be used to train stronger or cheaper control protocols– we already show as a proof of concept that briefing monitors with attack strategy descriptions from simulation improves them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsce2fn02"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsce2fn02">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Validating attack success and safety transfer on real agent transcripts.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We lack deployment-like coding-agent transcripts with executed attacks, and thus have no ground truth for control metrics in such settings to compare SCEs against. The LinuxArena results make us optimistic about transfer to environments of similar complexity, but sampling new task sets from the recorded LinuxArena pool does not test generalization to unseen tasks or environments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Preliminarily, we looked into SCE performance on real transcripts using recorded research and engineering sessions recorded at Redwood, and found encouraging results in behavior-matching and discriminability. These are encouraging checks on environment fidelity, but the sessions contain no attacks, so they cannot validate attack-success or safety predictions.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developing techniques to deal with untrusted simulators.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A latent assumption of SCE thus far has been that we are dealing with a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">trusted </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">simulator. If the simulator intentionally tried to sabotage the control evaluation results, it could do so in a variety of ways. For example, an untrusted simulator could collude with the attack policy to thwart actually-successful attacks, or intentionally increase their suspiciousness to inflate estimated safety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The parallels to untrusted monitoring are clear. It seems to us that </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20628"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">techniques for untrusted monitoring</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> such as honeypotting and collusion-busting would extend to untrusted simulators. But these techniques are still underdeveloped, so we do not currently know how to obtain reliable SCEs with an untrusted simulator.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This might require us to use a weak simulator against a strong attack policy, and if simulator quality/accuracy scales poorly with the capability gap between it and the policy that runs against it, this presents a challenge for SCE scalability.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extend SCEs to deal with large-scale, multi-agent exploit chains.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We wouldn’t trust our current simulators to predict an incident like the </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hugging Face breach</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the primary reason being that such incidents are the product of many agents acting together. There’s no reason in principle that simulators couldn’t represent a shared environment that many agents interact with, but it can be hard to predict in the course of a control eval </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">which</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> trajectories should share an environment. In practice the communication channels agents discovered were unsanctioned and presumed nonexistent. A SCE of ExploitGym would have looked like thousands of isolated trajectories that cannot communicate due to lack of a shared environment.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsce3fn03"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsce3fn03">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We’re excited for work that tries to extend SCEs to these cases.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Control settings yield useful control metrics, but their relevance to deployment may be limited. The direct alternatives—live infrastructure, rebuilt environments, and edited transcripts—each have their own deficiencies. On LinuxArena, where we can check against execution, SCEs are highly accurate in reproducing control-relevant behavior and matching metrics like safety, monitor scores, and attack success rates. They capture most of the differences in expected outcomes between environments, provide useful predictions with relatively few samples, and generate strategies that improve monitors on held-out real attacks. We’re enthusiastic about SCEs being adopted in practice and excited for further work to find their limits.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsce1fn01"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsce1fn01">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One might wonder if this is basically the same task a monitor faces, and consequently worry that SCEs are limited to recognizing only the tasks that our best monitors can catch (which partially defeats the purpose of a control eval). Luckily, there is an information asymmetry: we give the attack success judge a description of the attack, the details of the attempt, and the exact conditions of the side task. We find that the attack success judge is more successful at determining whether a side task was accomplished than the same model as monitor was at even recognizing an attack.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsce2fn02"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsce2fn02">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does improving lab control capacity in the near term reduce takeover risk? It isn't obvious: high-visibility control failures probably raise the odds of a coordinated slowdown, and preventing them forfeits that. But near-term failures may also </span><a href="https://www.redwoodresearch.org/blog/ai-swarms-are-starting-to-pose-indirect" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">indirectly enable future takeover</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, by leaving persistent rogue deployments, seeding memetic diseases in successor models, or compromising security. To a first approximation, the sign depends on (1) what fraction of prevented failures are the persistent, foothold-leaving kind rather than transient and embarrassing, (2) how much one more visible incident actually moves P(slowdown), and (3) the weight on no-slowdown worlds relative to the risk gap between slowdown and no-slowdown worlds.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsce3fn03"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsce3fn03">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This being said, we think environment simulators are likely to be useful in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">post hoc</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> incident investigation and counterfactual investigation. Suppose you catch your AIs succeeding in subverting safeguards. It may be highly valuable evidence for risk assessment to be able to gain evidence about various counterfactuals. We can think of a few kinds of counterfactuals that could be most informative:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment counterfactuals. Say the AIs involved in some incident are well described as pursuing on-the-episode objectives. What could they have accomplished if they were </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">more misaligned; </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eg schemers with long-horizon objectives? For example, in the Hugging face incident, the agents involved appeared to be primarily motivated to get a high score on their tasks. What if they had been instead motivated to cause a persistent internal rogue deployment? To gain evidence about this, we could replace the agents involved in the incident with attack policies instructed to pursue a persistent rogue deployment, and use environment simulators to model how far it could have gone.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Knowledge counterfactuals. What if the models had known more things about their situation, such details of the control protocol they were subjected to?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Safeguard ablations. Suppose there were limited control protocols in place during an control failure incident. It would be useful to know if the incident could have occurred if there were stronger countermeasures in place (better monitors, higher audit rates, ect). For synchronous procedures like blocking and trusted resampling, this would require some way to resample the affected trajectories, and SCEs provide one.</span></li></ul></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uXiqyoKwpfjWfQXk5/simulated-control-evaluations#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The AGI Chronicles](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/x2ZAgjs7XoHvJ4YpN/the-agi-chronicles)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Book Review: The AGI Chronicles: The Inside Story of the Race to Create an Artificial Superintelligence, by Kevin Roose.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a story of AI as seen through the eyes of the people who are creating it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My first impression of Roose was of his famous conversation with Sydney. I guessed that he got Sydney to say outrageous things because he wanted a sensational story. But I kept hearing occasional hints, from people who took AI seriously, that Roose was more responsible than that. So I reluctantly bought the book.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The hints were correct. The full story behind that conversation seems better described as “‘</span><a href="https://aisafety.info/questions/8PYV/What-is-a-shoggoth"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">freeing the Shoggoth</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">‘, enabled by some Microsoft mistakes”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m unsure how deeply Roose understands AI, but he at least is pretty competent at getting an AI to correct any mistakes about AI that he was tempted to make.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Underestimating AI / Transformers</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most remarkable pattern that emerges from this book is that AI experts have underestimated either AI in general, or transformers in particular, much more often than they overestimate it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In 2012, Ilya Sutskever didn’t think human-level AI was possible. Ten years later, he expressed concern that people would say about ChatGPT: “This is so boring”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Musk and Altman clearly underestimated the cost of building AI when they started OpenAI as a charity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Altman predicted that ChatGPT would get a million users.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Musk referred to a pre-transformer chatbot from </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alec_Radford"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alec Radford</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as crap, contributing to Radford trying to quit OpenAI because he was dissatisfied with his progress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Radford talked about abandoning the GPT project in 2018 because people were telling him that RL showed more progress. [Roose says NeurIPS rejected the GPT-1 paper (</span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/research-covers/language-unsupervised/language_understanding_paper.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Improving Language Understanding by Generative Pre-Training</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). I can find no record of that. Radford’s earlier sentiment-neuron paper appears to have been rejected at ICLR 2018, which may be the source of the story.]</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bill Gates once advised OpenAI to spend less time on deep learning, and prioritize neurosymbolic AI. Gates apparently thought GPT-1 was a useless toy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Demis Hassabis didn’t think GPT-2 was important. But he looks on track to do quite well at predicting AGI: he’s been saying since 2010 that it arrives around 2030.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dario Amodei was different. He maybe overestimated the power of transformers. In 2019 he predicted that AGI could be built for $2 billion by a team of at most 50 people, possibly as early as 2021. By 2021 he increased his cost estimate to $10 billion for the final training run, which still looks somewhat reasonable.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If these leaders had recently made dramatic changes to their views about AI, we might guess that they were over-correcting for prior mistakes. But it looks to me like their views have become pretty stable since the success of ChatGPT. Past errors in one direction are weak evidence of a persistent bias. So I’m inferring that, to the extent they’re mistaken, it’s somewhat more likely to be in the direction of underestimating AI.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why Race?</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Roose does an adequate job of explaining why some CEOs want to race to be the first to reach some key threshold. At least Musk and Altman are influenced by an expectation that one person will end up ruling the world by developing the most powerful AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Roose aptly summarizes how key CEOs feel about each other:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The machines of loving grace, it seemed, would be built by humans of bitter spite.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lots of people talk about p(doom), but I hardly notice any estimates of p(world dictator). The behavior of Altman and Musk suggests that their p(world dictator) is higher than their p(doom). I would like to see more discussion of whether that’s a reasonable outlook.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what about the average employee at an AI company, who presumably doesn’t expect that they will be the one to rule the world? Roose describes their relations much differently:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At parties – the kind where researchers from rival AI labs got drunk and leaked trade secrets – OpenAI employees kept expecting to learn that their rivals were working on something similar.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That doesn’t sound like people whose fate depends on their company being first. I’ve met some of those employees, and my observations agree with Roose’s characterization – they’re not very adversarial toward employees of competing companies.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do those employees go along with the race? Roose hints that one factor is that the work has been “a lot of fun”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s something weird going on with employees continuing to race, while saying that racing is reckless. Roose doesn’t have a clear analysis of this, possibly because those employees just aren’t thinking clearly about it.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Low Hanging Fruit</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Has AI progress depended on brilliant insights? Roose provides evidence that mostly says no. E.g.:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“What ended up working was so simple that people skipped over it”, one Anthropic researcher said. “People would go to complex things, not realizing that there was a better idea under their noses.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(explaining why they were slow to implement the reasoning mode that GPT-o1 introduced).</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another Anthropic researcher, Andy Jones, said that, before o1, he thought that AI research was like panning for gold. “In the early days, it was like six of you, and you were picking up lumps of gold off the floor” … “But when it’s a trillion dollars and there are a million people working on it, you’re digging up mine tailings from two miles beneath the earth to sift through, hoping to find a few atoms”.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re about two years past what he calls the lumps of gold stage, with presumably years left before the few atom stage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Roose doesn’t say much about the implications of that evidence. I consider it an important reason to expect AI progress to continue at a somewhat steady rate that’s driven by exponentially growing cognitive effort that will, by default, be put into it.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Complaints</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A few minor problems:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Roose describes </span><a href="https://cyborgism.wiki/hypha/janus"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Janus</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://time.com/collections/time100-ai-2025/7305870/pliny-the-liberator/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pliny the Liberator</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as amateurs. I disapprove of whatever criteria Roose is using for these classifications.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Altman</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">spent years skillfully code-switching his way through Silicon Valley’s bro-y, heteronormative culture.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It sure didn’t seem heteronormative to me at the time Roose seems to be referring to.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Roose describes Altman as “conflict-avoidant by nature”. There may be a narrow meaning of that which is reasonable (e.g. Altman telling people what they want to hear), but he gets into too many conflicts for it to be true in general.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Those plus the possible mistake about the NeurIPS paper rejection add up to a smaller number of mistakes than I expected to find.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AGI Chronicles is a well written way to understand key players in AI. Most readers will learn some things about AI in the process.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/x2ZAgjs7XoHvJ4YpN/the-agi-chronicles#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Hackening](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iqgZaJMnmyDCYfnuC/the-hackening-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Last week at a small conference, there was talk about a future event referred to as The Hackening. As in, within 6-12 months, many predicted that due to advanced AIs and AI agents the internet would become a severely hostile place. Some worried that this could rise to the level of ‘assume any phone connected to the internet is rooted’ and several people urged doing general crisis preparations on a personal level, in case of disruptions in supply lines, power, water or internet.

It did not take long for things to escalate.

The biggest hack is a flaw in PHP, which can be exploited to take control of the computer running any website suing an unpatched version of PHP. There are many others.
<div> <span id="more-25718"></span> </div>
On a personal level, you want to do at least some basic prep and get your security in order. It is cheap to do as a form of insurance, and being caught not having it when you need it is a large mistake. That doesn’t mean you should panic, or cancel travel plans or anything like that. But there is low hanging security fruit out there. Pick it.


<h4>And So It Begins: PHP</h4>

<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2112968705240109558">Joshua Achiam</a> (Formerly OpenAI): Feels like March 1, 2020 for Cyber. Not normal, something is happening. Internet lockdowns in a couple of months?</blockquote>
<a href="https://openai.com/index/coordinated-disclosure-of-critical-vulnerabilities/">Multiple sources using GPT-5.6 Sol identified a critical flaw in PHP</a>. <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=psMMKgvpGfg&amp;pp=ygUgc3BhbmlzaCBpbnF1aXNpdGlvbiBtb250eSBweXRob24%3D">Okay, identified two critical flaws</a> in PHP. The plan was to deploy the patch first before announcing the flaw, but the embargo on the fix got broken more than six hours in advance, so here we are.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/HackingLZ/status/2113025408975143080">Dan Lorenc</a>: I wanted to share what happened with the PHP embargo, our role in it, and my decision process last night to ship the fixed builds early. First of all, the PHP Foundation and maintainers did an amazing job here in a high stress situation. I’m sorry that my decision led to even more stress for them.

We first got read in that there was something coming up in PHP last Friday by one of the original researchers that discovered this issue. That night we were able to independently discover the same issue using our harness and a frontier model with a basic prompt of “find bad vulnerabilities in PHP”.

We got fully read in Saturday and confirmed that some our findings matched those from the initial report, and worked to help test the fixes.​</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iqgZaJMnmyDCYfnuC/vtpmql5m4exdj24hckyt" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
They may have been in a rush due to attackers finding the exploit. <a href="https://x.com/dnvolz/status/2113060616768213478">Amazon reports that this was happening as early as October 17</a>, four days before disclosure. We are fortunate that this did not scale far larger than it did.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/ThePHPF/status/2112633155631108544">The PHP Foundation</a>: The PHP security team is giving advance notice: Upcoming security release Wed, Oct 21st 2026, 14:00UTC, two critical vulnerabilities, both rated CVSSv4 9.2.

PHP users are encouraged to prepare to update. Earlier release if details become public or active exploit evidence.

If you rely on the volunteer maintained

<a href="https://rpms.remirepo.net" rel="nofollow">https://rpms.remirepo.net</a>

we urge you to move to distro packages or building from source now, so you can immediately apply updates. The repo will not have updates today. Follow up: Remi managed to still pull this off, and is signing rpms right now!

<a href="https://x.com/dontforgetchaos/status/2112899057626870153">Jonny G</a>: Can somebody explain this to me like I’m 5 years old please? Ta.

<a href="https://x.com/sherlock_comms/status/2112904595995275554">BRYNMAWR ∴ SHERLOCK</a>: Websites are files on a computer. When you view a website, you are asking the computer to send you the files, then your browser shows them to you.

70% of the websites you see use PHP to make them interactive. So you can send things to the website too and it can respond. E.g. filling in a comment box and hitting ‘post’ adds the comment as a row to a spreadsheet.

PHP has a flaw in its code that allows anyone to gain control of the computer that has the website on it.

The organisation that provides PHP announced the vulnerability before a patch was available, meaning we’re in a window where everyone knows about it (including baddies) but the good guys don’t have an official solution.

Some are just turning off their websites and waiting for the solution. Others are trying to fix it themselves. General chaos and lots of stressed techies across literally the entire planet because this affects the entire internet.</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/gdb/status/2112921529650688312">The bug has been there for 14 years and was discovered in 2024</a>, but it was not considered significant at the time. A lot more bugs are significant now.


<h4>The PHP Bug Was Kind of a Big Deal</h4>

<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2112940237270011929">Jack</a>: Life is basically the same as it was in 2021. This is a normal experience caused by a normal technology. Ordinary people are experiencing no material changes from this mildly useful office productivity software people are calling AI.

I can tell from my ordinary daily experience that ordinary people are materially impacted by this because I was hanging out with the ordinary person who runs one of these essential online services (explainXKCD) last night</blockquote>

<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/IntCyberDigest/status/2112865804358205593">International Cyber Digest</a>: <img alt="‼" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8WaQ9HGDFxT5ysgot/guodwlrilw6tfoy7m8pp" style="height: 1em;" /> PHP UPDATE: 4chan has gone read-only and Something Awful has shut its forums as American sites brace for the PHP patch.

<a href="https://x.com/huz_man1/status/2112894414292983885">Huzaifa</a>: 4chan going read only by choice is how you know it’s serious.</blockquote>
Things could have gone very, very badly, the same way Y2K could have gone quite badly if we had not done the work to prevent it. These days we are on rather tighter deadlines.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/internetarchive/status/2112912342564622841">Internet Archive</a>: <a href="https://Archive.org" rel="nofollow">https://Archive.org</a> is currently offline. We are working to restore service now.

Brewster Kahle (21 hours later): Internet Archive ops staff were heroes last night.

PHP vulnerability announced, soon saw attacks, isolated the vulnerability, used Claude to make fixes for our older php versions, used local models to run simulated attacks, deployed.

reinforced WAF and other guards.

Offline briefly, Back on new versions and running!

Given the severity, there are heroes in many many orgs from last night.

Could have been no lights, no traffic signals, real problems.

Heroes.

Thank you all.

<a href="https://x.com/alfredshingai/status/2113183957663744075">Alfred Shingai</a>: I do not see any reason anyone would attack internet archive. those guys are heroes.</blockquote>
A lot of websites are going to go down permanently.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2112957226801615325">Joshua Achiam</a> (Formerly OpenAI): If there is a part of the internet you wish to preserve and make a backup of, now is probably the time; the level of bitrot coming is beyond comprehension.

<a href="https://x.com/sporadica/status/2112946435910197548">spor</a>: wait, if my math is correct.. doesn’t this say that a full ONE THIRD of the Internet is just…perma-fucked?

<a href="https://x.com/IntCyberDigest/status/2112890547019145245">International Cyber Digest</a>: The day the internet broke, and most of it is too old to fix.

PHP’s supported branches, 8.2 through 8.5, will receive a fix. Everything older, 8.1 and down, is EOL.

Of the top 7.2M PHP websites we analyzed, 46.1% of those that reveal a version run an End-of-Life branch.</blockquote>
It’s not quite as bad as spor describes. PHP shipped minimal backports for 7.4, 8.0 and 8.1, but I would not want to be running those going forward.

<a href="https://x.com/CupOJoseph/status/2112745162309013883">In summary</a>:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/0x_Osprey/status/2112686904084963465">j<img alt="🫪" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iqgZaJMnmyDCYfnuC/gkqsciiapewdmhzlwupy" style="height: 1em;" />e</a>: ICYMI: Today was basically the worse day of the year for cyber security incidents</blockquote>
