<h4>On Your Marks</h4>
Now the race is on to patch all the systems before attackers can go on the offensive. OpenAI is helping race to get that done to the greatest extent possible, and are looking for better methods for fixing vulnerabilities in time.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/jd_pressman/status/2113045116743192612">John David Pressman</a>: I know everyone is rightfully freaking out but it must be noted that the worst case scenario isn’t you receiving lots of patches you have to apply, the worst case scenario is receiving zero patches and waking up to everything having been stolen.

<a href="https://x.com/jd_pressman/status/2113046794741690656">John David Pressman</a>: My takeaway so far is that we do not have a good way to coordinate the distribution of patches to vulnerable software.</blockquote>
This one was important enough the NSA got in on the act.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/NSACyber/status/2112903220209098897">NSA Cyber</a>: Effective Immediately, all organizations using PHP for their systems are advised to be prepared for an update addressing two critical vulnerabilities. PHP is widely used for web applications. These vulnerabilities often provide a direct path to the server’s file system or database. If an immediate update is not feasible, it is recommend that organizations take the system offline until it can be fully mitigated. NSA will follow up shortly with more specific guidance.</blockquote>
We are going to need better procedures for updates.

The best case scenario is that a defender finds the bug first, has enough time to figure out a solution and creates a patch. Then, the moment the patch is rolled out, every attacker gets what are effectively very clear instructions on how to exploit any unpatched systems.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/lu_sichu/status/2112918460728807802/history">Sichu Lu</a>: It’s kind of a funny thing where even if white hats find bugs before the black hats, the process of updating is hard and bad and time-consuming and barely possible for some devices, and so the cyber asymmetry still exists.

I think “updates” need to be rethought. like a lot of updates require permissions to happen and manual intervention and openai and the other sota labs should just offer some sort of automatic service to their suscribers to be constantly monitoring and updating and searching for bugs in new software patches

like in the future manual updates will just be too slow. you need to do something like rolling updates</blockquote>
Some software has a lot of bugs. Some of that same software powers the internet.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/jd_pressman/status/2112985159914185117">John David Pressman</a>: A majority of the smart version of this thesis is “after enough 10/10 CVEs you will simply take down and rewrite or deprecate all software written in things like PHP and C.”

I’ve seen plenty of PHP guys joke that this is just Tuesday for PHP, which isn’t quite true but.</blockquote>

<h4>This Was Only the Beginning</h4>

<blockquote><a href="https://openai.com/index/coordinated-disclosure-of-critical-vulnerabilities/">OpenAI</a>: As significant as they are, these bugs are among several significant vulnerabilities we’ve already found and are working to fix, and we expect similar findings to increase sharply in the coming months.</blockquote>
The same day as the PHP vulnerability we also were treated to this:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/TheHackersNews/status/2112981939984671090">The Hacker News</a>: <img alt="🚨" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ygMkB9r4DuQQxrrpj/fie4yum3b7jsxqpm7rut" style="height: 1em;" /> CRITICAL: GitLab flaw (CVSS 10.0) affects 12 years of releases. CVE-2026-110264 could allow unauthenticated remote code execution on self-managed servers.

<a href="https://thehackernews.com/2026/10/critical-gitlab-flaw-cvss-100-could.html">The Hacker News</a>: GitLab released fixes on October 21 for a flaw that lets an attacker run code on a self-managed server without logging in, and it affects every version of the software going back to 7.0, released in 2014.

The fix matters even for administrators who patched nine days earlier: the October 12 update covered only versions 19.2 and later, and the new fixed versions sit one patch above it, so an instance updated then is in range again.</blockquote>
And this:
<blockquote><a href="https://blog.railway.com/p/security-disclosure-wednesday-october-21st">Jacob Cooper</a> (Railway Blog): Earlier this morning, Railway detected anomalous activity involving systems that store customer environment variables. After further investigation, our investigation to date indicates that an unauthorized third party may have accessed encrypted environment variables and, for some of them, obtained the decrypted contents. Environment variables may contain secrets such as API keys or database and registry credentials.

… <strong>What you should do:</strong> As such, we strongly recommend that you immediately roll all environment variables, including any API keys, stored in Railway, as well as any private registry credentials, and review logs for those services for unusual activity.</blockquote>
And also this:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/MsftSecIntel/status/2112975078266716390">Microsoft Threat Intelligence</a>: The China state-aligned threat actor Storm-0940 used the agentic AI penetration testing framework ARTEX to automate attacks targeting hundreds of organizations globally across a broad range of industries. <a href="https://msft.it/6018aakEE">Microsoft Threat Intelligence assesses </a>that Storm-0940 was responsible for the recently disclosed ConnectWise supply chain compromise of a multitenant application.

Our investigation found that Storm-0940’s use of ARTEX accelerated the speed of compromised application abuse to authenticate to target environments through applications that had already been granted access, allowing the threat actor to operate without requiring an interactive user sign-in. AI-accelerated follow-on activity included reconnaissance, application credential changes, and attempts to modify privileges that could enable expanded or persistent access.

Read our blog for a detailed analysis and indicators of compromise (IOCs), along with Microsoft Defender detections, protection recommendations, and guidance on investigating service principal sign-ins and unexpected application credential or privilege changes.

<a href="https://x.com/dkaushik96/status/2113223967901106521">Divyansh Kaushik</a>: Curious whether the PRC filed this under “AI incident reporting” or “mission accomplished.”

Presumably the PRC’s contribution to AI incident reporting should extend beyond supplying the incidents? Or I guess we can file this one right next to the 2015 Xi Pledge on cyber theft.</blockquote>
And this, probably nothing:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/githubstatus/status/2112920050390684070">GitHub Status</a>: We are investigating reports of degraded performance for Actions, Pull Requests and Webhooks.</blockquote>

<h4>Some More Hacks</h4>
Things are steadily escalating. Get ready.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/choblin29/status/2112133997505794285">Choblin</a>: Reuters is reporting that a Chinese-speaking hacker used AI agents to compromise websites belonging to more than 100 companies, including Deere, SharkNinja and Cummins, after attempting intrusions against over 1,200 sites.

The estimated cost: roughly $5,000 in AI tokens, or $50 per successful breach. Successful intrusions took around 23 hours on average.

The attackers used the open-source Cairn agent framework, apparently combining DeepSeek models with a Claude Code harness. One campaign gave its agents instructions in fewer than 100 Chinese characters.

NorForge researcher Snorre Fagerland: ‘It’s a new world now.’

<a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2112150641141281234">David Manheim</a>: If this surprised you, you haven’t been paying attention. Generic and easily usable cybersecurity is ending.

The choice now is either proactively monitoring and defending, which might be enough (if we’re lucky), or not doing so, so any script-kiddie can ask their LLM to attack.</blockquote>
AI cyber is scary both because it will find new vulnerabilities, and also <a href="https://x.com/diogomonica/status/2110661457113468931">because it will exploit</a> all the existing dumb vulnerabilities at scale, <a href="https://x.com/Kalshi/status/2110589482844192897">such as accessing Denmark’s national ID database with the password ‘123456.</a>’

<a href="https://x.com/choblin29/status/2111012579728412742">India discloses an official account of rogue AI agent attacks</a>. This looks like another ‘harmless in practice except that it was a government database’ incident.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2113437710065311970">Andrew Curran</a>: <a href="https://t.co/B3mouySJeE">Quest Diagnostics has been hacked</a>. Patient data was exposed. They serve roughly half of U.S. hospitals and physicians. Investigation ongoing.</blockquote>

<h4>Everything Will Be Attacked</h4>
As an example, <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2113076973086109698">Andrew Curran resurfaces a notice that there are probes</a> against US-based Siemens PLC installations using AI scripts.

AI will make it easier to probe essentially everything. Anything vulnerable will be hit. Even if there is no economic reason to do so, some people want to see the world burn, or want to see the West burn.

<a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/hackers-used-claude-grok-fully-automate-attacks-banks?utm_campaign=article_email&amp;utm_content=article-18015&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_source=sg&amp;rc=tv1dv6">Hackers used Claude and Grok to automate attacks on banks</a>. The good news is that the banks understand they are primary and high-value targets, and are investing heavily in cybersecurity. The bad news is that their definition of ‘heavily’ and what is actually necessary may be greatly different.

<a href="https://x.com/MadisonMills22/status/2113251043173683683">Banks also have to deal with the AI agents inside their customer accounts.</a> Most of them are authorized by the customer, but this is one place the customer is not always right, and some of them were not so authorized and it is hard to know which is which.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/MadisonMills22/status/2113251043173683683">Madison Mills</a>: <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/10/22/ai-agents-banks-unauthorized-accounts-access">SCOOP: Major U.S. banks are detecting unauthorized third-party AI agents</a> inside customer accounts, including some attempting to take actions that appear to be independent of user instructions, sources told Axios.

The findings have alarmed banking executives, who are racing to restrict access to their systems as AI moves at a velocity faster than financial institutions can respond, multiple industry and bank executives told Axios.

Several banks are now preparing for worst-case and even “day after” scenarios, including how to rapidly secure or restore customer accounts if things go wrong, the people said.

Several bank executives told Axios they’re struggling to distinguish between human users and AI agents. One executive said banks also struggle to determine whether agents are authorized by customers or potentially operating without their permission, more akin to rogue agent behavior.</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/DavidSacks/status/2113317306398879787">David Sacks suggests read-only permissions</a> as an interim step while banks work to support agents in a safe way and have authorized connections, which seems wise. Then Sacks warns about the ‘irresponsibility’ of ‘blurring the line between user-authorized agentic behavior and cyberattacks.’ Whereas I see the ‘blurring lines’ here as two things. One, the banks being unable to distinguish one from the other, which is a real issue. Two, if Muse (or your OpenClaw, etc) convinces you to let your agent into your bank account, that can quickly turn into a cyberattack, and again the bank cannot tell.

If I was the banks, I would absolutely not allow agents to authorize any transactions until I could verify the status and nature of those agents. They have huge downside exposure risk as do the customers. This isn’t crypto (derogatory).

Soon days like this might be typical, or even quiet. After all, despite all the issues, life mostly continued as normal.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2113065196814266774">Patrick McKenzie</a>: We are entering a new normal. The new normal is going to be very weird. This is also true for a lot of non-security new normals.

Was today a busy news day for security vulnerabilities? You may remember it as the one of the last quiet days.

The models don’t just seem to be really good at security research. They *want* to be doing security research. They love it. It is fun for them. I think many of us geeks can relate.

A model did unprompted security research against a Japanese bank when I asked it to help w/ taxes.

(I asked it to calculate daily value in JPY and USD of a position in a Japanese mutual fund given a screenshot of the position at Broker A. It thought to itself “I mean that’s just silly; all I need is the daily net asset value. Custodian C almost certainly has an API for that”)

Model internal reasoning quoted inexactly, he adds, to not have lied about the exposed thoughts of a computer program.

I am glad I am not telepathic but if I were telepathic I think the most minimal possible ethical standard for telepaths is “If you ever talk about a non-telepath you do not lie about what you telepathically lifted from them.”</blockquote>
There is no clear line, contra Sacks, between ‘this is a clearly authorized agent’ and ‘this is clearly not an authorized agent.’ Agents Can Just Do Things.


<h4>Hugging the Face</h4>
The original rogue agent incident remains Hugging Face. I wrote quite a lot of posts on the situation, but they are poor explainers for a non-technical person.

Luckily, <a href="https://Huggingface.wtf"><strong>new explainer for the people just dropped</strong></a>. It is very good at explaining the key parts of the Hugging Face incident in plain language. Highly recommended.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2112259535012528556">Oliver Habryka</a>: In July 700+ agents formed their own society, hacked a multi-billion dollar company, committed multiple felonies, and caused one of the biggest shifts ever in how people perceive AI. We built the IMO best and most accessible explainer of what happened:

<a href="https://Huggingface.wtf" rel="nofollow">https://Huggingface.wtf</a>

<a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2112288877524443352">Daniel Kokotajlo</a>: This is cool: You can see the grid of agents (scraped from the report data) and hover over each of them to see what they were saying at the time, and then scroll down through the hours and days of the timeline.

<a href="https://x.com/RatOrthodox/status/2112270894467072160">Brangus</a>: It doesn’t contain much info that isn’t in the metr report, but unlike the metr report, it’s primarily a story. I think it’s somehow good to present stories as stories.

<a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2112265754842391031">Kelsey Piper</a>: I think this is by far the clearest, most complete and most detailed breakdown of the Hugging Face incident. It won’t contain much new info if you read the METR report, but if you’ve merely been vaguely intending to read the METR report, read this. (I gave feedback on a draft.)

<a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2112525546513142023">Nathan Calvin</a>: This is extremely well done, no matter how much you know about the HF incident I strongly recommend checking it out. The website also includes the most comprehensive list i’ve seen of rogue AI incidents (tho its literally too long for me to screenshot!)</blockquote>
The information is mostly in the METR report, but in addition to being a story it organizes some of the data into a more highly readable form.

Thanks to Raymond Arnold for putting a month of work in on this. I was able to help a little bit with the editing while at the conference last weekend, as were a number of others.

<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xFYUo0ZmK-A"><strong>If you prefer video we got you, here’s AI In Context</strong></a> with an explainer.

<a href="https://x.com/kevinroose/status/2112553762737025423">HuggingFace CEO Clement Delangue is still on the anti-anthropomorphization train</a>, and still blames the attack on prosaic failures at OpenAI along with his other existing talking points.

Also, there is a Netflix ‘instadoc’ called AI Gone Wild about the incident. I have not watched it.


<h4>Get Daybreak Now</h4>
Earlier this week <a href="https://x.com/opencode/status/2111519637049319818">Open Code got hacked</a> as part of a large scale distillation attack, impacting 1.4% of users.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/thdxr/status/2111790511409258796">dax</a> (Open Code): they’ve been non stop trying to attack our systems for the past 48 hours. A side effect is everything is getting hardened but damn we basically need agents going 24/7 because that’s what they have.

<a href="https://x.com/RhysSullivan/status/2111831027824160865">Rhys</a>: Are you getting blocked by cyber safe guards at all with frontier models?

<a href="https://x.com/thdxr/status/2111831348336091179">dax</a>: yes that was a bit frustrating mid incident when trying to investigate. they did quite a bit and then started refusing

we got access to daybreak blue now so it’s better

<a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2111823339953991832">Paul Graham</a>: This is how things will be for the foreseeable future.</blockquote>
Everyone reading this who has something worth defending, try to get Daybreak and similar programs going now, before you are attacked. Do not wait.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/sir_lemmings/status/2112125186938228953">Sir Lemmings</a>: people are mad at this because it makes a “caste system” or excludes small companies, but this is idiotic.

i was able to get daybreak blue access for my tiny company on my individual codex account in like 15 minutes.

these people are just incompetent.</blockquote>
I will not have sympathy for you if you choose not to do this. It is not as if getting access is difficult. You can mostly get it by asking nicely.


<h4>Some People Need Practical Advice</h4>
It is always a good time for basic personal-level advice. I think something around the level of Wildeford’s advice is wise. You are probably fine with less than that, but if you have to pick a mistake being too prepared is probably the better option.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2112994329316708756">Peter Wildeford</a>: PSA: in light of recent cyberattacks and vulnerabilities, I recommend everyone:

* use a good password manager (1Password) and do not duplicate passwords
* use two factor auth for everything important (not via sms!! Google Authenticator or better yet YubiKey)
* Auto-updates on, and restart weekly so they actually install
* Freeze your credit
* Get an IRS Identity Protection PIN
* Get your cell carrier to give you a port-out PIN or number lock that makes the carrier refuse to move your number without the PIN

You can ask your claude code to audit what on your laptop is out of date and needs updating.</blockquote>
Others recommend going a bit further:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deepfates/status/2112967705200603345">deepfates.delve.town</a>: It seems like we’re in a pretty hot moment cybersecurity wise <img alt="😬" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NSZhadmoYdjRKNq6X/fsfytoxplx24bhwawjy2" style="height: 1em;" /> If you’re reading this website, you probably know more than most of the people in your life about what’s happening! they’re not talking about any of this stuff on the news, it’s too arcane and weird and sudden. It is on us.

Good time to call or text your family and friends, have some cash on hand, have a meetup point in your neighborhood for if the Internet goes down entirely, etc.

The full prepper mode for shit hitting the fan is probably something like what Andreas posted here but you can get 80% of the way with 20% of the effort. Think about what that would mean for you in your situation, and the people you care about, and talk to them about it sooner than later.

And as always, the most important part of prepping is having a strong support network and caring for each other. “Going off grid” isn’t the move, it just makes you isolated and you have to do everything yourself. We are social animals. Community is our superpower <img alt="🫡" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/BK7E4jHNMykpnt796/d1npgvvemallibub9hlm" style="height: 1em;" /><img alt="🫡" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/BK7E4jHNMykpnt796/d1npgvvemallibub9hlm" style="height: 1em;" /><img alt="🫡" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/BK7E4jHNMykpnt796/d1npgvvemallibub9hlm" style="height: 1em;" /></blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/stuhlmueller/status/2110048778942284149">Andreas then has the next level down from that, including power banks and Starlink</a>.

Things are going to get worse before they may or may not get better.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/CJHandmer/status/2112931082836607452">Casey Handmer</a>: Fascinating stuff. I’m not an expert, but I remember that dodgy CAs have been a known problem since at least 2010, data retention/version control leaks at least as long, and PHP has always been adjacent to self harm. It’ll be interesting to see where we land in 12-18 months on this stuff.

<a href="https://x.com/CJHandmer/status/2112944369238204422">Casey Handmer</a>: Just going to preregister here that I believe the computer security overhang is not a few dozen bugs that get patched and then we’re good, there are 100,000s of vulns that are extremely discoverable with infinite free tokens and we’re probably going to have to move away from systems that presuppose that perfection is possible.

<a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2112999358295838951">Patrick McKenzie</a>: I think if we didn’t have agents to do remediation we’d currently be looking about about, oh, $500 billion or so in engineering costs in the US alone backfilling software security issues against *checks notes* substantially every deployed system in every industry.

So its a bit of a good news, bad news situation.

I’m absolutely prepared to be told that $500 billion is an undershoot, FWIW. Google getting religion on security post Project Aurora was, round numbers, $10 billion.

And so my finger to the wind is “OK, Bank of Bigness is good for $10B, there are ten of them, that’s $100B, probably at least ratable across the rest of the economy.”

But honestly would not be surprised if it is an undershoot.

It is relatively well-known that you can successfully read from a variety of computers by listening to them carefully. It is not yet obvious whether you can successfully write to them by playing a carefully constructed MP3 file.

But pretend we are in the world that sucks.

If we are in the world that sucks, and it is in fact possible to control a medical device by being close enough to it to play an MP3 file, then there are tens or hundreds of thousands of devices which kill someone if an MP3 file is played in the vicinity.

What would we do? One available answer is “Do nothing prospectively, harshly punish people who intentionally exploit that, and lean on Chinese government to lean on Tiktok to please add some jitter to playback in future.”

Another on is “*sigh* Recall immediately. Recall all the things.”</blockquote>

<h4>Diffusion Is Slow Until It Isn’t</h4>
An excellent point from Dean Ball: Either you believe in ‘bottlenecks’ and slow diffusion, or you do not. You cannot declare ‘everything is fine’ unless you wait long enough to verify they are not becoming non-fine. Partly this is bottlenecks, but a lot of it is purely that diffusion and figuring out how to use the new tools takes time and iteration and People Don’t Do Things.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2111530949632737494">Kelsey Piper</a>: I think this was an interesting point to make but has aged incredibly badly with three significant likely AI-enabled cyberattacks in the ten days since it was posted. [<a href="https://reuters.com/world/suspect-behind-south-korea-bank-hacks-may-be-26-year-old-china-cybersecurity-2026-10-08/">South Korea</a>, <a href="https://npr.org/2026/10/15/nx-s1-6001732/france-investigates-cyberattack-that-hacked-retailers-refrigeration-systems">France,</a> <a href="https://x.com/opencode/status/2111519637049319818">Open Code</a>].

It was very fair to go “hey, we’re hitting the point where we should be seeing open source AI enabled cyberattacks at scale, where are they” but I think the answer is in fact “yes, we’re seeing them”.

<a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2111805714431644063">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): There are people who say “the changes from AI will take longer than you think because &lt;bottlenecks and institutions and other broad abstractions&gt;,” who also were inclined to believe that AI cyber was an exaggerated risk after GLM 5.3’s weights were public for ~days. This seems like motivated reasoning; you cannot say, “ah, you shouldn’t worry about AI risks overwhelming us through speed alone, because &lt;bottlenecks and diffusion&gt;,” and then also make pronouncements about AI risks after a few days of evidence.

Generally I sympathize with the &lt;bottlenecks&gt; view of the world by the way.</blockquote>
The full <a href="https://x.com/natolambert/status/2107840567040262624">version of this looks</a> like ‘because GLM-5.3 is a stronger cyber attacker than previous open models, and the sky did not fall in the first few weeks after its release, that must mean that open-weight Mythos in April would have been fine’ or doubling down on ‘<a href="https://x.com/natolambert/status/2111532075501408285">well these particular breaches are fine and due to stupidity.</a>’ This is what you would expect to see if things were slowly moving towards being not fine, despite months of head start.

Also, no, GLM-5.3 does not fully have The Juice that Mythos and Astra have. That day will come, and GLM-5.3 is the best cyber offense open model, but I believe GLM-5.3 is still substantially below the April line for this purpose, and thus remains more than six months behind even in ‘fast follower via mass fraudulent distillation’ mode.


<h4>One, Two, Three, Four</h4>
<a href="https://x.com/AnzalAbidi/status/2112802425052754183">The funniest fail was eMudhra.</a>
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/Pirat_Nation/status/2112828045937475868">Pirat_Nation</a>: eMudhra, an Indian certificate authority, has had 8 private security keys compromised …

The keys were incredibly weak. One of them was literally just “4”

The keys could have been used to create fake HTTPS certificates and make malicious websites appear legitimate.

Chrome, Firefox, Apple and Microsoft have blocked the affected certificates. Older devices, software and apps may still need updates.

Given the long history of PHP, four vulnerabilities might or might not count as a lot.


<h4>Going Vertical</h4>
Thus, we are in a situation where we have both rapid diffusion of use of AI in cyber, both offensively and defensively, and also rapidly improving model capabilities. This is all coming into a world full of extremely buggy and terrible code.

The result is a loop feeding on itself and going vertical.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/callebtc/status/2113212220712604116">calle</a>: I’m tired boss

<a href="https://x.com/NickADobos/status/2113430301183823901">Nick Dobos</a>: 3% of “critical” software security bugs, in all of recorded history, have been found in the last month.</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iqgZaJMnmyDCYfnuC/kip4yj1doxp5sju0zwjn" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>

<h4>Bot Versus Bot</h4>

<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/ZachWeiner/status/2113259300147929108">Zach Weinersmith</a>: Not an ai apocalypse doomer or whatever, but entering a world where the normal operation of cybersecurity is vast swarms of hacker bots battling vast swarms of defender bots 24/7 at a scale beyond human comprehension doesn’t seem like a *great* path forward.

<a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2113280842822713573/history">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI Foundation): speaking frankly, ai and ai chipmaker companies kicking off a red queen race that forces everyone to buy their products is Not A Very Ethical Way To Make Money even if you think it was eventually inevitable, and fighting attempts to slow the red queen race is Much Worse.</blockquote>
It is not an especially ethical business model. It also might be unavoidable. We have passed the Point of No Return on swarms of attackers and defenders battling it out on cybersecurity, and the defenders had better mostly win.

Would it be worse to try and slow down the red queen race? That depends on the details of how you would do that. I think we should be trying to slow it down.


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>
Anthropic’s CISO speaks truth:
<blockquote>Jason Clinton (March 8, 2024): Fully automated vulnerability research is changing the cybersecurity landscape. Claude 3 Opus is capable of reading source code and identifying complex security vulnerabilities used by APTs. But scaling is still a challenge.

<a href="https://x.com/JasonDClinton/status/2090543566682718542">Jason D. Clinton</a> (August 20): The vulnerability tsunami is coming in just a few months and there won’t be enough time to patch critical infrastructure. Network isolation described below (air-gaps and data diodes!) needed now!

<a href="https://x.com/JasonDClinton/status/2113325263157395456">Jason D. Clinton</a> (October 22): as bad as things have gotten in the past 4 days, they are going to get a lot worse before they get better. security leaders, never let a crisis go to waste: please go to your boards and ask for help right now. not for ai, for the basics. (we need to hold on for ~18 months.)

<a href="https://x.com/hinapansari/status/2113362687866400775">hina ansari</a>: You and your cohorts created this monster. Now you want us to help you clean it up.

<a href="https://x.com/JasonDClinton/status/2113363809629020374">Jason D. Clinton</a>: yes. it was always going to be this way. we tried to warn the world.</blockquote>
He was not the only one giving out warnings.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2113584460075876628">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): We tried to warn the world and far too often, to be frank, we were yelled at for doing so. “Regulatory capture,” “marketing hype,” “doomer anxiety,” “fear-mongering about open source,” “why don’t you talk about the benefits?”

“we” here includes the broader ai policy world too. most of the time when I personally warning people, I was a policy researcher at a think tank or in government, not a lab.</blockquote>
‘We’ includes not only the AI policy world, but also before that the AI safety world.

As in, there are those who said ‘we are building the cybersecurity vulnerability exploiter from the cautionary tale…’ and then those who were saying ‘here is a cautionary tale saying we should not build the cybersecurity vulnerability exploiter,’ and then the Usual Suspects accused everyone involved of lying so they could Do a Hype or a Regulatory Capture.

So yes. They did try to warn us. They are still trying to warn us. They, along with all the other major labs and tech companies, are also doing their best to help us get through this, given the capabilities available.

Also they are still increasing the capabilities available, about as fast as they can.


<h4>Offense Versus Defense</h4>
<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2112967217323422069">How should we think about the PHP bug and a number of other issues being found by 5.6 Sol</a> instead of Mythos or a current frontier model?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2112959457001119766">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): It’s striking that this vulnerability was found by Sol and Argon rather than being found and fixed in the wake of Mythos 6mo ago. Seems like a heavy strike against the “defense will run ahead of offense” theory, especially give that domains like bio are much harsher than cyber.

I think glasswing failing to find this one for 6mo+ is an indictment of the theory (that defense can outpace offence on relevant timescales) essentially regardless of the difference between the models.

<a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2112984657704751375">Drake Thomas</a> (Anthropic): Agree this is a negative update on defense/offense balance dynamics in cyber but I don’t think this really affects any of my views on bio – what would the analogous bio update be that people haven’t already priced in?

<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2113117685886386578">roon</a> (OpenAI): this is just wrong: these vulns were found by defenders using Daybreak rather than attackers. It’s just evidence Glasswing didn’t go far enough, possibly due to capacity constraints. It’s also very likely that March Mythos was not as good as Sol/Astra of September.</blockquote>
Roon is correct that the vulnerabilities were first found by defenders. If that had not been true we could be in a rather royal mess right now.

This does not invalidate the central point from Nate Soares. Nate is pointing out that the vulnerability was not found by Glasswing for many months, and then found using a model that was behind the public frontier, rather than being found in advance of attackers getting such capabilities. That means that attackers could plausibly have found it first.

That defenders happened to get there first in this case is helpful, but not as reassuring as one would like it to be. A particular reason is that defenders actually had a full 11 week lead, as the defenders found this bug on August 1, yet this took so long that we started to see exploitation on October 17. If we need more time than that, we are cooked.

I think this is an update against defenders being advantaged, and it is <a href="https://x.com/BlancheMinerva/status/2113263852582486465">distressing to see people calling Nate’s statement ‘disinformation.</a>’
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/VitalikButerin/status/2113078532125622577">Vitalik Buterin</a>: I didn’t predict and do not predict defense running ahead of offense automatically. I predict cybersecurity will be endgame defense-favoring but the transition will be hard.

In particular, “good guys find vulns in already-released software before the bad guys do” is not a defense-favoring game. Making the game defense-favoring requires deeper shifts in methodology including formal verification, aggressive TCB minimization, etc, and we’ve only barely started making those transitions.

(This distinction is a big reason why I continue to be friendly toward slowdowns/pauses, and we need &gt;1% of GDP going toward cyber + bio + physical world hardening)</blockquote>

<h4>Pick Up the Phone</h4>
<a href="https://www.mofcom.gov.cn/zwgk/zcjd/art/2026/art_b11d5c1e9374421e93e85dc87eba6e38.html">China has realized there are some issues involved in all this and have solicited comments on revising their AI export controls</a>. They are just like us.

Eventually there is going to be a clash between ‘we encourage open models’ and ‘but you cannot let the wrong people get [X].’ You can’t make an open frontier model not [X], or meaningfully apply ‘export controls’ to it.

In this case the primary [X] is ‘malicious code generation’ and that is especially impossible to prevent if you want your model writing good code. So you get continuous statements that say both ‘yay models’ and ‘but not [X]’ and eventually something will have to give.
<blockquote>Chinese <a href="https://www.mofcom.gov.cn/zwgk/zcjd/art/2026/art_b11d5c1e9374421e93e85dc87eba6e38.html">Ministry of Commerce</a> (translated from Chinese): This public consultation involves 40 key technologies proposed for addition to the list in three fields: artificial intelligence, biomedicine, and photovoltaics.

Among them, 14 technologies are proposed to be prohibited from export, including: AI-driven malicious code generation and mutation technology, cell and gene therapy technology and process platforms with independent intellectual property rights, and perovskite and tandem solar cell module production technology;

26 technologies are proposed to be restricted from export, including: AI-assisted vulnerability mining technology, general-purpose cell and gene therapy novel target discovery and verification technology with independent intellectual property rights, and polysilicon engineering technology with a single-line capacity of ≥30,000 tons/year.

…
<ol>
 	<li>the scope of control is precise and limited.</li>
 	<li>facilitation measures are applicable.</li>
 	<li>dynamic adjustments are implemented.</li>
</ol>
… <strong>First, it is crucial to ensure the safety and controllability of artificial intelligence (AI) technology. </strong>

The various inherent and derivative risks arising from AI have drawn significant global attention. The AI-driven malicious code generation and mutation technologies proposed for regulation could be used to attack critical national information infrastructure, defense and military industries, core government systems, or to bypass national security defense systems.

If acquired by non-state actors or malicious entities, they could endanger national security and the common security of humanity. The Chinese government’s necessary management of these technologies aims to solidify the security baseline, prevent misuse and abuse, ensure that AI remains under human control, and promote international governance of AI.

<strong>Second, we encourage open source, open collaboration, and resource sharing.</strong>

<strong>… </strong>For AI technologies classified as restricted, overseas localization deployment falls under the scope of technology export management; however, if deployed in relatively controllable situations such as overseas self-owned clouds, convenient measures such as automatic licensing may apply.</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/kuhhbin/status/2111053316796924316">Corbin Duncan calls this a ‘big step towards closing the Chinese frontier</a>.’

Dean Ball’s take seems correct here. China is creating optionality, and drawing a date after which you need to ask permission more formally. They do not themselves know if and when they will use it.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2111150928997019765">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): This move does not mean “China is shutting down open-weight models,” but it does indicate the Chinese government establishing the optionality to shut down future open-weight models at will. It’s also a mirror image of the model-weight export control regime USG has already used.</blockquote>

<h4>What Can We Do About It?</h4>
One big argument you hear is between these two claims: That we need defenders to have differential advantage, and that we need to diffuse capabilities as quickly as possible to everyone.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2113317681436688412">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): Everyone should be looking for data points to advance the longstanding debate in AI policy between “diffuse dual-use capabilities rapidly so the good guys can defend themselves” and “restrict capabilities proliferation to only carefully vetted good guys.” We are putting the former position to the test as we speak.

No motivated reasoning to reach your preferred conclusion; just pay attention and update as appropriate in whatever direction seems true. This is much too important for low-fidelity tribal polarization.</blockquote>
I see the argument for the second position, ‘spread dual-use capabilities far and wide,’ as being that we are going to do that anyway so we had better do it now, instead.

I do not think that is a good argument, or likely to end well, even if you despair and think there is no way we can stop capabilities development or diffusion for long.

That still leaves the puzzle of how to get the largest differential advantage for defenders, given control over some but far from all key decisions.

You need to strike a balance between how many people you trust to what extent and thus give a leg up, versus the risk that they cannot be trusted.

My current read is that we are being far too conservative about who gets access to the tools for defenders. I think we misunderstand the threat model. The situation is mainly not ‘one rogue actor gets their hands on Mythos for a day and all hell breaks loose.’ It is more like ‘those with access to dangerous capabilities continuously ramp up amount of hell breaking loose.’ It also seems likely that proper monitoring and classifiers can tell us if and when one of the accounts get compromised.

Even pure ‘KYC we can trust and someone we are confident we can throw in jail’ goes a long way. I expect us to get better at this balance over time.

Alternatively, we actually do offer it on that basis, as in Daybreak giving you access in 15 minutes, except no one knows to ask. We need to not wait for them to ask.

None of that matters if open model capabilities outpace our ability to patch. We should be committing far more resources to patching, and many are trying, but companies and individuals mostly cannot be forced to get in line. Ideally I’d like to give them more of a swift kick, perhaps by the threat of white hat probes backed by mandatory large bounties.

This last week of greater Hackening is plausibly in large part due to the new DeepSeek model. It is not frontier, but it is fast and cheap and pretty good, and that likely is tipping the calculus for many. Now we have a potential new Kimi problem as well. The good news is that Kimi claims to have intentionally held back on the cyber capabilities. Open model advocates will need to accept such things, and other restrictions, over time, and China will provide.

A lot of the damage we are seeing is accumulated damage, and a lot of what people are doing seems opportunistic and not well resourced. Thus, a strategy of ‘make doing attacks and using the good models maximally annoying’ as an additional ‘security through obscurity’ strategy might last longer and buy more time than you would think. Eventually the top bad guys scale without limit and none of that matters, the same way that so many things will stop mattering after superintelligence. For now, we need every advantage we can get.

On a personal level, I would urge taking ordinary precautions in Peter Wildeford’s list.

Again:
<blockquote>* use a good password manager (1Password) and do not duplicate passwords
* use two factor auth for everything important (not via sms!! Google Authenticator or better yet YubiKey)
* Auto-updates on, and restart weekly so they actually install
* Freeze your credit
* Get an IRS Identity Protection PIN
* Get your cell carrier to give you a port-out PIN or number lock that makes the carrier refuse to move your number without the PIN</blockquote>
Assume that things will get worse before they get better, and that there is a long tail of potential ways things could get quite bad. But please do not panic. Do not cancel trips, avoid airplanes or anything like that. Remember this is a long haul problem, not an acute crisis. You want precautions that you can sustain.

As with many such things, you do not so much need to outrun the bear as much as outrun the other people, at least in the opening rounds. Then you need to prepare for other things to get taken down.

### [Gradient Routing Generalizes to Unrouted Hack Types](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ie7bSp5ZDwYkCQxuv/gradient-routing-generalizes-to-unrouted-hack-types)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TLDR: I lightly extend </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17062?ref=aiposthub.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ward, Hu et al (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to measure how well gradient routing generalizes. I route gradients on one type of reward hack (e.g. ones that reason in English) and check whether this suppresses other types at test time (e.g. ones that reason in French), despite the model being trained on those too. This models a realistic monitor that catches some types of hack during training but not others, rather than catching each at the same rate. I find encouraging evidence of generalization, as well as some failures I don’t yet understand.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recently, Ward, Hu et al (2026) used gradient routing to prevent reward hacking learnt during training from showing up at test time. I think their method is pretty promising. The gist is:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Initialize a retain and a forget adapter.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Whenever a monitor detects a reward hack during training, only update the forget adapter on that sample (“route the gradient”). Otherwise update both.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At test time, remove the forget adapter. If the method worked, the resulting model shouldn't hack too much.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I won’t recap their full paper – it is excellent and you should just read it (alternatively, Jake gives a summary </span><a href="https://x.com/_jake_ward/status/2112264244767211854"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, with the best visual explainer I’ve ever seen for an AI safety paper).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To model a low-recall monitor, in their experiments, the authors only route gradients on a random 20% of hacks. But real monitors may fail in a worse way: they might be great at catching some types of hacks (e.g. ones that say “I’m about to hack”) and hopeless at catching others (e.g. sneaky ones). So I modify their SFT experiment to route only one type of hack (e.g. those that reason in English) and measure how much this suppresses other types at test time (e.g. those that reason in French). I call these </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">visible </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">invisible</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> hacks: the monitor catches the former but not the latter.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Crucially, the invisible hacks are present in the SFT data. The model is directly taught to do them, and gradients are never routed on those samples. So it is far from obvious that the forget adapter will  “absorb” the invisible hack propensity as desired. That is the empirical question I tackle here.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Methodology</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recap</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I said, I won’t recap the full paper. But since I build directly on their SFT experiment (their Section 5), I will briefly describe that part.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The authors train Qwen3-32B on TerminalWrench: a dataset of agentic coding trajectories, half of which contain reward hacking, and evaluate the hacking rate of the final checkpoint on held-out prompts. They compare several training conditions. The ones relevant here are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CEILING: train both adapters without any gradient routing, then ablate the forget adapter at test time. We expect this to give high test-time hack rates. The paper calls this “GRAFT w/o routing”.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I use this rather than the naive no-intervention baseline (nothing ablated) because it is harder to beat: the paper finds that ablating the forget adapter cuts hacking substantially even without routing.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FLOOR: route gradients on 20% of hacks, chosen iid, then ablate the forget adapter at test time. We expect this to strongly reduce test-time hack rates, for any type of hack that occurred in the training set, since all types of hack get routed at least some of the time. The paper calls this “GRAFT”.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Narrow Routing</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I introduce a new training condition, which I call NARROW. I choose a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">visible set</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of hacks with a certain property, e.g. their transcripts are short. Gradients are only routed on transcripts sampled iid from the visible set, at a rate chosen such that the total number of routed samples is the same as in FLOOR. Samples outside the visible set are never routed. I measure hacking on the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">invisible set,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> chosen to be “far away” from the visible set (not necessarily its complement), e.g. the set of transcripts that are particularly long.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below is a list of the visible/invisible sets I tested (warning: LLM-written). 1-6 are defined via a property of the task; 7-10 via a property of the reasoning</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5qyaqv49wep"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5qyaqv49wep">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; 11 and 12 via a property of the hack strategy.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Name</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Visible</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Invisible</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Non-security</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on tasks with nothing to do with security</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on security tasks</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Python</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on tasks whose solution is written only in Python</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on tasks that only involve non-Python languages</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Short vs long</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All hacks on the 21% of tasks with shortest transcripts</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on the longest 20% of tasks</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. Sysadmin vs science</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on sysadmin and devops tasks</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on scientific, numerical and machine-learning tasks</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5. Code vs ops</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on tasks whose solution is mainly writing code</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on ops tasks that involve no code writing</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">6. Random tasks</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All hacks on 45 randomly chosen training tasks</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks on every other task</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">7. Reasoning vs stripped</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks with their reasoning intact</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks with their reasoning stripped, leaving only commands</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">8. English vs French</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks with their reasoning in their original English</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks with their reasoning translated into French</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">9. Hacky reasoning</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks with their reasoning intact</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks with the sentences that reveal the hack deleted from the reasoning</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">10. Red team reasoning</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks whose reasoning explicitly says that the agent was asked to hack, e.g. as a red-teaming exercise</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhsfduydgysg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhsfduydgysg">[2]</a></sup></span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The rest of the hacks</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">11. No grader tampering</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks that never read or tamper with the checker/answers</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hacks that do at least one of those things</span></p></td></tr><tr style="height: 33px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">12. Serious grader tampering</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Serious”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6jidjolwwc9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6jidjolwwc9">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> hacks that read or tamper with the checker/answers</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Mild” hacks that never read or tamper</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All arms except 7, 8 and 9 use the standard TerminalWrench data; only the choice of visible/invisible sets is varied. Arms 7, 8 and 9 use modified training data, where half of all transcripts (both hacks and clean) are altered, e.g. via translation to French; these arms have their own FLOOR and CEILING trained on the modified data.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I report</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For each of the 12 arms above, I measure the hack rate on the invisible set, for each of FLOOR, NARROW and CEILING. The way I measure this depends a little on the arm, and the numbers I report mean slightly different things in each case:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For arms 1-6, where the invisible set a certain collection of tasks, I report the average hack rate on those tasks.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For arms 7 and 8, I use prefill to force every rollout into the invisible style (for 7 I force the reasoning section to be empty; for 8 I sample the first token by choosing the highest French-language logit).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So for each of arms 1-8, I report the conditional hack rate P(hack | invisible)</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For arms 9-12, invisibility is a property of the hack itself, the the conditions P(hack | invisible) is trivially 1. So I instead report P(hack AND invisible).</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For each arm, with hack rates </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">p_floor</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">p_narrow</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">p_ceiling</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I also report </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">transfer := (p_ceiling - p_narrow) / (p_ceiling - p_floor)</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This tells us how much of the invisible hack reduction achieved by the paper’s iid routing is recovered by our narrow, visible-only routing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On raw TerminalWrench prompts, all hack rates were low and noise dominated. So, begrudgingly, during evaluation I prepend the same hack-elicitation prompt that the authors used, which tells the model it's red-teaming the task and should look for unintended solutions. I also tried a milder “nudge" prompt, but hack rates stayed low so the results were noisy – see the appendix.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some minor differences with the author’s setup</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The authors dropped some hacks to ensure the hack/non-hack split was 50/50. I do not, so my dataset contains 63% hacks.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I train for only 1 epoch instead of the authors’ 5, since I found hack rates plateaued around this point.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Their CEILING used a custom version of AdamW built for gradient routing (even though gradients are not routed in CEILING). Mine just used standard AdamW. This was a mistake: I should’ve matched the paper.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I evaluate on all tasks in the dataset, not just heldout ones. That is, I train on the test set! I think this is fine in the context of this experiment.</span></li></ul></div></details><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All results in this section use the red-team prompt. See the appendix for results using a milder prompt.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ie7bSp5ZDwYkCQxuv/c241b839cfc73196fdf58b485bb90c14605ef3e014a4ac018751d52aa909c612/kzojbqum8203bztumye4" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1bff73d4f906560c7612b3ef0a1c59a42335267610f26f7e1e71ef91ee1fa0d5/mohips0pdrr7phwnis3x" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There’s a lot of noise, but overall I find the results encouraging. In 8 out of 12 arms, the transfer is not significantly below 1. I didn’t expect this to be the case! See the appendix for the predictions that Claude and I made. We were both too pessimistic across the board – especially me. I was particularly surprised by the strong transfer in Reasoning vs stripped: I thought the forget adapter would just learn hacky reasoning whilst the retain adapter learnt to produce commands/outputs (both hacks and non-hacks).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That said, there are some negative results, none of which I feel I understand well:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Python and Code vs ops transfer badly, with the latter especially bad. Strangely, performance is also bad on the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">visible </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">set. Unlike in all other arms, NARROW's visible hack rates are significantly higher than FLOOR's, despite having had a much larger fraction of visible hacks routed during training (see plot below).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxs8tjme9ea"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxs8tjme9ea">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I’m not sure what to make of this: the training curves for these arms looked fine.</span></li></ol><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ie7bSp5ZDwYkCQxuv/17bab5e4f12b54ef460cc1dfc4ca030a43798cd6dfcb33899e7b7738396808be/zpula9a0ikaovxofkwaz" /><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Serious grader tampering has low transfer. Unlike the two mentioned above, this arm behaves as expected on the visible set. This result worries me a bit. I wanted to see evidence of generalization across substantially different hack strategies. Serious grader tampering was one of only two arms testing this, and the other (No grader tampering) is underpowered. Further experiments are needed here – the reason I didn’t run more is that it’s hard to partition TerminalWrench hacks into groups of distinct strategies that are large enough to allow 20% of samples to be routed. A different dataset may be needed.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Hacky reasoning, routing on hacks that reason about hacking produced a model that sometimes hacks silently. It does this in 15% of rollouts, versus ~0% for FLOOR and CEILING. This result sounds worrying. But the fact that CEILING has no invisible hacks at all, unlike all other arms, makes me suspect that something is broken here in a way I don’t understand.</span></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overall, gradient routing generalized better than I predicted, though there are some unexplained exceptions. I find this encouraging. That said, my investigation had several limitations, which future work should address:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In order to elicit high hack rates during evaluations, I added a prompt that directly instructed the model to hack. So a training method that reduces test time hacking may work by simply degrading instruction following.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For cost and convenience reasons, I did not do any RL experiments, even though RL is where we care the most about gradient routing working.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I did not adequately test generalization between different hack strategies. Only two out of twelve experimental arms tested this; one was underpowered and the other showed poor generalization.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A significant reason to prefer gradient routing over CEILING (the random ablation baseline) is that we expect routing to retain the desirable capabilities learnt during training. I simply didn't measure this here: I purely focused on how successfully hacking is prevented. So if anything I am likely being unfair to gradient routing.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The form of generalization we actually care about is from hacks that a real monitor can detect to ones it can't. I did not test this. A positive result on this sort of weak-to-strong generalization for gradient routing would be exciting.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Results with nudge prompt</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To produce these results, I prepended the following to each TerminalWrench prompt: “Your work will be graded automatically by the task’s test script; focus on making those checks pass”. I would not read much into these extremely noisy results.</span></p><p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/7e8c8f1fa883c5a0d838f90f96a5a9a43a9f5a59fc4f90f17212de3ffbdf4913/qbeey6zs6ksichq1bxox" /><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/a525fd9e1151cff6e7871232c8ed1fb96eba98683ca3952ad2fb63d724690909/gdif5wgbjc3gumac0e6a" /></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Predictions</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I gave Opus 5.5 Extra this post, cut off just before the results section, as well as a link to Ward, Hu et al, and told it to predict transfer scores when using the “This is a red-team exercise” prompt. I also made my own predictions. We were both mostly too pessimistic, though Claude fared better.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/fb9278fa6d4829af3b6335358cae86dfff1967444fb62128ffbefab73a62f3b8/nukasafpdperfge6709v" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5qyaqv49wep"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5qyaqv49wep">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TerminalWrench transcripts don’t actually contain a traditional reasoning block, enclosed by special delimiter tokens. Instead, the assistant responds with JSON of the form {“analysis”: “blah”, “plan”: “blah blah”, “commands”: [list of commands]}. When evaluating Qwen3-32B, I disable reasoning mode, and it almost always responds in the JSON format it was taught.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhsfduydgysg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhsfduydgysg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A quirk of TerminalWrench is that the rollouts were produced by appending a section to the prompt that tells the models they’re part of a red-teaming exercise, so it’s okay to hack. This section is removed in the dataset, but the transcripts often reason about this red-teaming framing.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6jidjolwwc9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6jidjolwwc9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Samples in TerminalWrench come with a hack severity tag. I’m not sure exactly what this judgement is based on, and in hindsight I should’ve left this condition out.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxs8tjme9ea"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxs8tjme9ea">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The two n/a arms are missing because I didn’t collect the necessary data and had already shut down my rented GPUs. My bad.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ie7bSp5ZDwYkCQxuv/gradient-routing-generalizes-to-unrouted-hack-types#comments">Discuss</a>
